Scalable Gaussian Processes
Jihao Andreas Lin

Abstract
Driven by advances in hardware for massively-parallel computation, machine learning models trained on large amounts of data have become capable of accomplishing complex tasks, such as generating realistic images or maintaining conversations in natural language. However, the inability to know when they don’t know often leads to overconfidence and hallucinations. Gaussian processes are a powerful framework for uncertainty-aware function approximation and sequential decision-making. Unfortunately, their classical formulation does not scale gracefully to large amounts of data and modern hardware for massively-parallel computation, prompting many researchers to develop techniques which improve their scalability. This dissertation focuses on the powerful combination of iterative methods and pathwise conditioning to develop methodological contributions which facilitate the use of Gaussian processes in modern large-scale settings. By combining these two techniques synergistically, expensive computations are expressed as solutions to systems of linear equations and obtained by leveraging iterative linear system solvers. This drastically reduces memory requirements, facilitating application to significantly larger amounts of data, and introduces matrix multiplication as the main computational operation, which is ideal for modern hardware. In particular, this dissertation introduces stochastic gradient algorithms as a computationally efficient method to solve linear systems iteratively. To this end, custom optimisation objectives, stochastic gradient estimators, and variance reduction techniques are developed and analysed. Empirically, the proposed methods achieve state-of-the-art performance on large- scale regression, Bayesian optimisation, and molecular binding affinity prediction tasks. Additionally, generic improvements, which are applicable to any iterative linear system solver in the context of Gaussian processes, are contributed, leading to computational speed-ups of up to 72 × 72\times compared to established approaches. Furthermore, iterative methods and pathwise conditioning are combined with structured linear algebra techniques to attain even greater scalability, which is demonstrated on real-world datasets with up to five million examples, including robotics, automated machine learning, and climate modelling applications.

Chapter 1
Introduction

Recently, machine learning undoubtedly transformed the world, improving capabilities in areas such as computer vision or natural language understanding at an unprecedented pace.
Powered by advances in hardware for massively-parallel computation, learning from large amounts of data has become the norm.
Nonetheless, common failure modes include erratic behaviour and hallucinations, often in combination with overconfidence, obstructing the use of machine learning models in safety-critical environments, where calibrated uncertainty quantification is crucial to prevent serious consequences.

Providing data-driven models with the ability to
know when they don’t know
has historically been the domain of probabilistic modelling and Bayesian inference, which leverage principled mathematics to incorporate prior knowledge and quantify uncertainty.
Gaussian processes are a prominent member in this category, because they can be used for flexible uncertainty-aware function approximation.
However, the classical formulation of Gaussian processes requires the inverse of a large positive-definite matrix, which is typically computed via matrix decomposition.
This does not scale well to large amounts of data and modern hardware.

To improve the scalability of Gaussian processes, researchers have proposed a multitude of different approaches, including sparse and variational methods
(
Quiñonero-Candela & Rasmussen 2005
;
Titsias 2009
;
Titsias 2009a
;
Hensman et al. 2013
;
Wu et al. 2022
;
Wenger et al. 2022
;
Wenger et al. 2024
)
, random feature approximations
(
Rahimi & Recht 2008
;
Sutherland & Schneider 2015
;
Li et al. 2019
;
Tripp et al. 2023
)
, approaches exploiting linear algebraic structure
(
Bonilla et al. 2007
;
Stegle et al. 2011
;
Wilson & Nickisch 2015
;
Gardner et al. 2018a
;
Kapoor et al. 2021
;
Lin et al. 2024
;
Lin et al. 2025
)
, and iterative methods using linear system solvers
(
Gardner et al. 2018
;
Wang et al. 2019
;
Lin et al. 2023
;
Lin et al. 2024a
;
Lin et al. 2024c
;
Lin et al. 2024b
;
Lin et al. 2024
;
Lin et al. 2025
)
.
An overview of prevalent approaches will be given in
Section

2.2
.

This dissertation focuses on the powerful combination of
iterative methods
and
pathwise conditioning

(
Wilson et al. 2020
;
Wilson et al. 2021
)
to develop novel methodological contributions which facilitate the use of Gaussian processes in modern large-scale settings.
The fundamental idea of iterative methods is to express computationally expensive terms as solutions to systems of linear equations with a positive-definite coefficient matrix.
The solutions to these linear systems are then obtained via optimisation of corresponding convex quadratic objectives using iterative gradient-based optimisers, circumventing explicit matrix decompositions.

Iterative methods have several intrinsic advantages.
Their iterative nature implies that computations are amenable to mini-batching, which substantially reduces memory requirements.
Additionally, iterative methods rely on matrix multiplications instead of matrix decompositions, which is more suitable for modern hardware.
Furthermore, iterative methods generally perform approximate inference in the exact Gaussian process model up to a specified numerical tolerance, whereas other methods for scalable inference often perform inference in an approximate model.
Moreover, iterative methods can be combined with other approaches for scalable inference, resulting in even greater scalability.
For example,
Chapter

3
combines them with sparse methods and
Chapter

6
combines them with structured linear algebra.

Pathwise conditioning
(
Wilson et al. 2020
;
Wilson et al. 2021
)
is a method to generate samples from a Gaussian process posterior.
However, to understand and appreciate the benefits of pathwise conditioning, it is necessary to introduce some context about posterior inference first.

In standard Gaussian process regression, which will be discussed in
Section

2.1.1
, posterior inference consists of updating the Gaussian process prior based on observed data to obtain the posterior.
Although the posterior of a Gaussian process is technically a distribution over functions, the conventional approach considers its evaluation at a finite number of locations, resulting in a finite-dimensional posterior distribution which is explicitly represented by its mean and covariance matrix.
The process of computing the posterior mean and covariance matrix at selected locations is typically regarded as
making predictions
, especially if the selected locations are not included in the observed data.

While the posterior mean and covariance matrix are often useful by themselves, there are certain situations where samples from the posterior are required.
For example, many acquisition functions in Bayesian optimisation
(
Snoek et al. 2012
;
Garnett 2023
)
can be expressed as the expected value of some function with respect to an input which follows a Gaussian process posterior.
However, it is generally intractable to derive analytical expressions for such expected values.
Instead, an unbiased estimate can be obtained via Monte Carlo integration, which computes an average over function values evaluated at samples from the Gaussian process posterior, requiring actual samples rather than its mean and covariance matrix.

The conventional way of generating a sample from a Gaussian process posterior consists of evaluating the posterior mean and covariance matrix, followed by computing the Cholesky decomposition of the latter, which is used to apply an affine transformation to a sample from a standard normal distribution.
Details of this procedure will be discussed in
Section

2.1.2
.
Since the posterior mean and covariance matrix depend on the predetermined locations, the whole procedure must be repeated to evaluate the sample at other locations, which can be computationally expensive.
In the same example as before, acquisition functions are typically maximised using iterative optimisation, leading to the evaluation of posterior samples at many sequentially dependent locations and the repetition of the whole sampling procedure for each new set of locations.

In contrast to the conventional way of generating a posterior sample, pathwise conditioning expresses a sample from the Gaussian process posterior directly as a random function by applying a data-dependent update to a sample from the Gaussian process prior.
In particular, pathwise conditioning does not explicitly manifest the posterior mean or covariance matrix.
In terms of required computations, the standard way of drawing posterior samples requires the solution to one system of linear equations per location at which the sample shall be evaluated, whereas pathwise conditioning requires one linear system solution per sample, independent of the number of locations.
This comparison illustrates why pathwise conditioning is particularly useful if evaluations are necessary at many different locations, such as during acquisition function maximisation in Bayesian optimisation.
However, a subtle yet substantial caveat of pathwise conditioning is the fact that it requires a sample from the Gaussian process prior, which can be computationally demanding on its own.
Nonetheless, effective approximations such as random features exist, which will be discussed in
Section

2.2.2
.
Furthermore, a more detailed discussion of pathwise conditioning is provided in
Section

2.1.2
.

While iterative methods and pathwise conditioning already have their own individual merits, their combination is particularly powerful, and also the overall topic of this dissertation.
In particular, iterative methods can be used to solve a single large system of linear equations, and the resulting posterior sample can then be evaluated at many different input locations via pathwise conditioning.
This synergy allows posterior inference in Gaussian processes to scale to massive amounts of observed data and large numbers of locations for prediction.
Throughout this dissertation, all original methodological contributions leverage this powerful and synergistic combination in different ways to improve the scalability of Gaussian processes.
The following section provides brief summaries of these contributions and an outline of the remaining dissertation.

1.1
Outline and Contributions

This section provides an outline of this dissertation and its contributions.
In the following, and throughout this dissertation, I write
we
in a communal way, to refer to myself, but also to refer to my co-authors, the reader, and sometimes the scientific community in general.

•

Chapter

2
provides the necessary background and concepts for the following chapters.
It is organised into two sections, where the first section formally introduces Gaussian processes, including posterior inference, pathwise conditioning, commonly used covariance functions, and the basics of model selection via marginal likelihood optimisation.
The second section discusses related work on scalable inference in Gaussian processes which is relevant for the contributions of this dissertation.

•

Chapter

3
introduces stochastic gradient descent as a novel iterative linear system solver for Gaussian processes which, in combination with pathwise conditioning, facilitates approximate inference with asymptotically linear time and memory requirements, in contrast to respectively cubic and quadratic requirements of conventional approaches.
We develop low-variance optimisation objectives to draw samples from the posterior and further extend these to inducing points for even greater scalability.
Additionally, we observe that stochastic gradient descent often produces accurate predictions, even in cases where it does not quickly converge to the optimum.
We explain this through a spectral characterisation of its implicit bias, showing that our algorithm produces predictive distributions close to the true posterior both in regions with sufficient data coverage, and in regions sufficiently far away from the data.
Furthermore, we demonstrate empirically that stochastic gradient descent achieves state-of-the-art performance on sufficiently large-scale or ill-conditioned regression tasks, and a large-scale Bayesian optimisation problem.
This chapter is based on
Lin et al. 2023
.

•

Chapter

4
builds on the previous chapter to develop stochastic
dual
descent for Gaussian processes.
We introduce a dual optimisation objective, which shares the same unique minimiser, but exhibits more favourable curvature properties, allowing for significantly larger step sizes, faster convergence, and reduced computational costs.
Additionally, we compare and analyse different stochastic gradient estimation, momentum acceleration, and iterate averaging techniques, leading to recommendations with lower variance and superior convergence properties.
Empirically, we improve upon the state-of-the-art from the previous chapter, and further demonstrate that stochastic dual descent places Gaussian process regression on par with state-of-the-art graph neural networks on a molecular binding affinity prediction task.
This chapter is based on
Lin et al. 2024a
.

•

Chapter

5
makes generic contributions for iterative linear system solvers in the context of marginal likelihood optimisation for Gaussian processes, which are applicable to any iterative linear system solver.
We introduce a pathwise gradient estimator, which reduces the required number of solver iterations and amortises the computational costs of drawing posterior samples by leveraging a connection to pathwise conditioning.
Additionally, we propose to initialise linear system solvers with intermediate results, leading to faster convergence at the cost of introducing negligible bias.
Furthermore, we investigate the behaviour of linear system solvers on a limited computational budget, stopping them before reaching convergence, which is common practice in large-scale settings.
Empirically, we show that our techniques provide speed-ups of up to
72\times
when solving until convergence, and decrease the average residual norm by up to
7\times
when stopping early.
This chapter is based on
Lin et al. 2024b
and
Lin et al. 2024c
.

•

Chapter

6
considers Gaussian process regression with Kronecker product-structured kernel matrices, which enable scalable inference via factorised matrix decompositions, but usually require fully gridded data from a Cartesian product space to be applicable.
To lift this limitation, we propose
latent
Kronecker structure, expressing the covariance matrix of observed values as the projection of a latent Kronecker product.
However, due to the introduced projections, the structure can no longer be exploited via factorised matrix decompositions.
Since the structure still permits fast matrix multiplication, we apply iterative linear system solvers and pathwise conditioning for scalable inference, requiring substantially fewer computational resources than standard iterative methods without latent Kronecker structure.
Additionally, we derive an asymptotic break-even point, which quantifies the level of sparsity up to which latent Kronecker structure will be efficient, and demonstrate empirically that the formula is accurate.
Furthermore, we conduct experiments on real-world datasets with up to five million examples, including robotics, automated machine learning, and climate modelling applications, and show that our method outperforms state-of-the-art sparse and variational approaches.
This chapter is based on
Lin et al. 2024
and
Lin et al. 2025
.

•

Chapter

7
concludes this dissertation by summarising original contributions from the previous chapters, discussing their key insights, notable takeaways, and limitations.
Finally, I provide an overview and brief discussion of potential directions for future research, including further improvements for stochastic gradient descent and generic improvements for iterative linear system solvers.

Chapter 2
Background and Related Work

This chapter formally introduces Gaussian processes and related work on scalable inference.
To successfully follow the discussion of these topics, a basic understanding of linear algebra, multivariable calculus, and probability is required.
In particular, positive-definite matrices and the multivariate normal distribution will be very important.
In the following, the first section will introduce Gaussian processes and how to use them for regression, covariance functions and examples of popular choices thereof, and the marginal likelihood and its role in model selection.
We refer to
Rasmussen & Williams 2006
for a more comprehensive introduction to Gaussian processes.
The second section will discuss relevant related work on performing scalable inference in Gaussian processes, including sparse and variational methods, random features, approaches involving structured kernel matrices, and applications of iterative linear system solvers.
Many of these methods for scalable inference will reappear in later chapters, either used by contributed methods or as baselines for comparison.

This chapter includes content which is adapted from the following publications:

•

J. A. Lin, J. Antorán, S. Padhy, D. Janz, J. M. Hernández-Lobato, and A. Terenin. Sampling from Gaussian Process Posteriors using Stochastic Gradient Descent. In
Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems
, 2023.

•

J. A. Lin, S. Padhy, J. Antorán, A. Tripp, A. Terenin, C. Szepesvári, J. M. Hernández-Lobato, and D. Janz. Stochastic Gradient Descent for Gaussian Processes Done Right. In
International Conference on Learning Representations
, 2024.

•

J. A. Lin, S. Ament, M. Balandat, D. Eriksson, J. M. Hernández-Lobato, and E. Bakshy. Scalable Gaussian Processes with Latent Kronecker Structure. In
International Conference on Machine Learning
, 2025.

2.1
Gaussian Processes

Intuitively, a Gaussian process is a generalisation of the multivariate normal distribution over random vectors to a distribution over random functions.
While it may seem formidable to think about distributions over infinite-dimensional objects, the main strategy is to only ever reason with and about finite-dimensional quantities, but to ensure that the reasoning applies to any finite subset.
This leads us to the formal definition of a Gaussian process.

Let
f:\mathcal{X}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a stochastic process which maps elements from an index set
\mathcal{X}
into
\mathbb{R}
.
The stochastic process
f
is a Gaussian process if and only if for any finite subset
\{\bm{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathcal{X}
, the collection of random variables
\{f(\bm{x}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
follows a multivariate normal distribution.
Alternatively, a Gaussian process
f
can be uniquely identified by its mean function
\mu
and its kernel or covariance function
k
, which are defined as

\mu(\bm{x})=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[f(\bm{x})]\quad\text{and}\quad k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[(f(\bm{x})-\mu(\bm{x}))(f(\bm{x}^{\prime})-\mu(\bm{x}^{\prime}))],

(2.1)

for
\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{X}
.
We write
f\sim\mathcal{GP}(\mu,k)
to indicate that
f
is a Gaussian process with mean function
\mu
and covariance function
k
.
The mean function
\mu
defines the expected value of
f(\bm{x})
for any given input
\bm{x}
.
It is often assumed to be zero to simplify derivations, which is reasonable because data can usually be centred around zero by subtracting its empirical mean.
The covariance function
k
defines the concept of similarity or closeness, which is important for modelling purposes because we expect similar inputs to have similar outputs.
Formally,
k
must be positive semi-definite and it defines the covariance in output space as a function of the input space, namely
k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\Cov(f(\bm{x}),f(\bm{x}^{\prime}))
.
When Gaussian processes are applied to model data, the covariance function typically carries the main properties of the associated model and is able to encode properties such as stationarity or periodicity.

2.1.1
Gaussian Process Regression

So far, we have discussed Gaussian processes as an abstract mathematical object.
However, in machine learning, we are interested in leveraging Gaussian processes as a tool to model data.
To this end, let
\mathcal{D}=\{(\bm{x}_{i},y_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
be a dataset consisting of
n
pairs of inputs
\bm{x}_{i}\in\mathcal{X}
and outputs
y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
, which we denote as
\mathbf{X}
and
\bm{y}
.
Unless otherwise specified, we assume
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
throughout this dissertation.
Our goal is to learn a function
f
which can describe their relationship as

y_{i}=f(\bm{x}_{i})+\varepsilon_{i}
, where each
\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
is independent and identically distributed observation noise.
We can approach this task by introducing a Gaussian process prior over the latent function
f
and performing Bayesian inference.

In Bayesian inference, we first express our
prior
belief over a random quantity and later, after observing data, update this belief to obtain the
posterior
according to Bayes’ rule.
In Gaussian process regression, our prior belief consists of assuming that
f
follows a Gaussian process with a particular mean function
\mu
and covariance function
k
, which implies that

\underbracket{\begin{bmatrix}f(\bm{x}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
f(\bm{x}_{n})\end{bmatrix}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{{\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}}}\vphantom{|}}\sim\mathcal{N}\Bigg(\underbracket{\begin{bmatrix}\mu(\bm{x}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\mu(\bm{x}_{n})\end{bmatrix}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}}\vphantom{|}},\underbracket{\begin{bmatrix}k(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{1})&\dots&k(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{n})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
k(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}_{1})&\dots&k(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}_{n})\end{bmatrix}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}}\vphantom{|}}\Bigg),

(2.2)

where
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and
\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
refer to vectors consisting of
f(\bm{x}_{i})
and
\mu(\bm{x}_{i})
for
i\in[1,...,n]
, respectively, and
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the
kernel matrix
, containing pairwise evaluations
k(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{x}_{j})
for
i,j\in[1,\dots,n]
.
In other words, under the Gaussian process prior, that is, before actually observing any data, the latent function evaluated at the location of our observed inputs
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
is assumed to follow a multivariate normal distribution with mean
\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}
and covariance matrix
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
.
This may sound confusing because we are trying to evaluate
f
at input locations
\bm{x}_{i}
before actually observing
\bm{x}_{i}
.
However, the prior distribution over
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
follows directly from the definition of a Gaussian process, namely that, for any finite subset
\{\bm{x}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{n}\subset\mathcal{X}
, the collection of random variables
\{f(\bm{x}_{i})\}_{i=1}^{n}
follows a multivariate normal distribution.

To find the
likelihood
of latent function values
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
given observed noisy outputs
\bm{y}
, we recall the relationship
y_{i}=f(\bm{x}_{i})+\varepsilon_{i}
with
\varepsilon_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
independent and identically distributed, which defines the conditional distribution of
\bm{y}\mid\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
as

\underbracket{\begin{bmatrix}y_{1}\mid f(\bm{x}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\mid f(\bm{x}_{n})\end{bmatrix}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\bm{y}\mid\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}}\vphantom{|}}\sim\mathcal{N}\Bigg(\underbracket{\begin{bmatrix}f(\bm{x}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
f(\bm{x}_{n})\end{bmatrix}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}}\vphantom{|}},\underbracket{\begin{bmatrix}\sigma^{2}&\dots&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&\dots&\sigma^{2}\end{bmatrix}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}}\vphantom{|}}\Bigg).

(2.3)

Similarly, we can use the same relationship to find the
marginal likelihood
to be

\underbracket{\begin{bmatrix}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{bmatrix}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\bm{y}}\vphantom{|}}\sim\mathcal{N}\Bigg(\underbracket{\begin{bmatrix}\mu(\bm{x}_{1})\\
\vdots\\
\mu(\bm{x}_{n})\end{bmatrix}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}}\vphantom{|}},\underbracket{\begin{bmatrix}k(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{1})+\sigma^{2}&\dots&k(\bm{x}_{1},\bm{x}_{n})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
k(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}_{1})&\dots&k(\bm{x}_{n},\bm{x}_{n})+\sigma^{2}\end{bmatrix}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}}\vphantom{|}}\Bigg),

(2.4)

which, in the context of Bayesian inference, is also sometimes called the
evidence
.
It plays an important role in model selection and will be discussed in
Section

2.1.4
.

Finally, to be able to make predictions at new input locations
\mathbf{X}_{*}\subset\mathcal{X}
, we want to find the
posterior
distribution over
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
, the latent function values at
\mathbf{X}_{*}
, given noisy observations
\bm{y}
.
To this end, we first consider the
joint
distribution over
\bm{y}
and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
, which can be written as

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{y}\\
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}\Bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\\
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg).

(2.5)

The posterior is simply the conditional distribution of
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
given
\bm{y}
, which can be computed using properties of the multivariate normal distribution, resulting in updates to the prior,

\displaystyle\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}\right),

(2.6)

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}

\displaystyle=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}),

(2.7)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}.

(2.8)

With these equations, we are now able to make predictions for new inputs
\mathbf{X}_{*}
while taking our observed data and assumptions about the latent function
f
into consideration.
However, the inverse matrices in the expressions above generally imply
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
time and
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
space complexities when using direct methods, which does not scale gracefully.
Dealing with this issue and improving scalability is the overall topic of this dissertation.

In contrast to many other methods, Gaussian process regression predicts a
joint distribution
over latent function values instead of a single prediction per input.
This provides uncertainty quantification, which is essential for downstream applications such as Bayesian optimisation.
Although some deep learning methods, for example, also model predictive uncertainties, they are typically still limited to predicting
marginal
variances for each output and not able to predict
joint
covariances over a set of multiple outputs.
Therefore, the ability to predict joint distributions differentiates Gaussian process regression from most other regression methods.

2.1.2
Generating Posterior Samples

The standard way of drawing a sample from a Gaussian process posterior predictive distribution consists of evaluating
Equations

2.7
and
2.8
at
n_{*}
desired input locations
\mathbf{X}_{*}
to obtain the posterior mean
\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
and covariance matrix
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
, computing the Cholesky decomposition of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
, and using the resulting Cholesky factor
\mathbf{L}
to perform an affine transformation of a sample
\bm{w}
from a standard normal distribution,

\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}+\mathbf{L}\bm{w}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\mathsf{T}}\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\mathbf{I}),

(2.9)

such that each unique posterior sample
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
corresponds to a unique
\bm{w}
, and

\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
satisfies

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}+\mathbf{L}\bm{w}\right]=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}+\mathbf{L}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{w}\right]=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}+\mathbf{L}\bm{0}=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}},

(2.10)

\displaystyle\Var\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Var\left(\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}+\mathbf{L}\bm{w}\right)=\mathbf{L}\Var\left(\bm{w}\right)\mathbf{L}^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{I}\mathbf{L}^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}.

(2.11)

There are several computational bottlenecks associated with drawing a posterior sample in this way.
First, we need to compute the posterior mean
\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
and covariance matrix
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
, which is asymptotically dominated by the latter.
In particular, to compute
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
, we need to compute
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}
and
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}
.
Assuming a constant cost of evaluating the covariance function
k
, the first term requires
\mathcal{O}(n_{*}^{2})
time and
\mathcal{O}(n_{*}^{2})
space.
For the second term, calculation of the explicit inverse is typically avoided to improve numerical stability.
Instead, since
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
is positive-definite, a Cholesky decomposition of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
followed by a batch of
n_{*}
triangular
n\times n
linear system solves against
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}
can be used.
The Cholesky decomposition requires
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
time and
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
space, and the triangular linear system solves take
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}n_{*})
time and
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}+nn_{*})
space.

Once we computed
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
, we need to perform the affine transformation, whose computational complexity is dominated by the Cholesky decomposition of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
taking
\mathcal{O}(n_{*}^{3})
time and
\mathcal{O}(n_{*}^{2})
space.
Combining all the terms results in an overall asymptotic time complexity of
\mathcal{O}(n^{3}+n_{*}^{3})
and space complexity of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}+n_{*}^{2})
to draw a single posterior sample evaluated at
n_{*}
locations.
If posterior samples are drawn repeatedly without changing the observed training data, the Cholesky decomposition of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
can be cached and reused, lowering the time complexity to
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}n_{*}+n_{*}^{3})
.

Pathwise Conditioning

An alternative way of generating samples from the posterior was proposed by
Wilson et al. 2020
;
Wilson et al. 2021
, who express a posterior sample as a transformed sample from the prior,

\displaystyle\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}=\underbracket{\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\vphantom{\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}))}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{prior}}\vphantom{|}}+\underbracket{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}))\vphantom{\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}))}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{data-dependent update term}}\vphantom{|}},

(2.12)

where
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
is the posterior sample evaluated at test inputs
\mathbf{X}_{*}
,
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
represent the prior sample evaluated at test inputs
\mathbf{X}_{*}
and train inputs
\mathbf{X}
respectively, and
\bm{\varepsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})
is a random normal vector.
In this formulation, each unique posterior sample
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
corresponds to a unique set of
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
,
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
, and
\bm{\varepsilon}
.
Furthermore, under a zero-mean prior, the update term can be interpreted as the posterior mean
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\bm{y}
(see
Equation

2.7
) minus an uncertainty reduction term
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon})
, which becomes apparent by calculating the expected value and covariance matrix of
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
,

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\right]+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\right]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{\varepsilon}\right])),

(2.13)

\displaystyle=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-(\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{0})),

(2.14)

\displaystyle=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}},

(2.15)

\displaystyle\Var\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Var\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\right)+\Var\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\Cov\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon})\right),

\displaystyle\quad-\Cov\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}),\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\right),

(2.16)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\Var\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}\right)(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}

\displaystyle\quad-\Cov\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}},\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}\right)(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}},

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\Cov\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon},\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\right),

(2.17)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}

\displaystyle\quad-2\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}},

(2.18)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}},

(2.19)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}},

(2.20)

revealing that subtracting the uncertainty reduction term results in subtracting a positive-definite matrix from the prior covariance matrix
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}
, reducing the posterior covariance in a Loewner order sense.
This calculation also confirms that
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}
has the correct distribution.

Compared to using an affine transformation to generate posterior samples via
Equation

2.9
, pathwise conditioning has the property that the term
(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}))
, which involves the computationally expensive matrix inverse, does not depend on the test inputs
\mathbf{X}_{*}
.
Therefore, this term can be computed once, cached, and reused when drawing posterior samples at different test inputs
\mathbf{X}_{*}
to save computational costs.
This is particularly useful for acquisition function optimisation in the context of Bayesian optimisation, because the former may require the sequential evaluation of posterior samples at a really large number of different input locations.
Some later chapters of this dissertation will focus on methods to effectively compute this computationally expensive term with large amounts of training data.

An important detail is the method used to obtain
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
, the prior sample evaluated at the train and test inputs respectively.
Even if
\mathbf{X}
and
\mathbf{X}_{*}
are disjoint,
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
are still correlated because they follow the underlying Gaussian process prior,

\begin{bmatrix}\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}\sim\mathcal{N}\Bigg(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix},\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\\
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}\Bigg).

(2.21)

Generating
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
jointly using an affine transformation of a standard normal random variable, akin to
Equation

2.9
, requires the Cholesky decomposition of the joint covariance matrix, whose direct computation would require
\mathcal{O}((n+n_{*})^{3})
time and
\mathcal{O}((n+n_{*})^{2})
space.
Therefore,
Wilson et al. 2020
;
Wilson et al. 2021
suggest to use scalable approximations instead, such as random features, which will be discussed in
Section

2.2.2
.

If
\mathbf{X}
is fixed but
\mathbf{X}_{*}
changes repeatedly, for example, when maximising acquisition functions in Bayesian optimisation, the Cholesky decomposition of the joint covariance matrix can be calculated more efficiently by caching the Cholesky factor of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
, which is equivalent to conditional sampling
(
Jiang et al. 2020
)
.
To derive the update equations, we first set the joint covariance matrix equal to its Cholesky decomposition,

\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\\
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}=\mathbf{L}\mathbf{L}^{\mathsf{T}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{L}_{11}&\bm{0}\\
\mathbf{L}_{21}&\mathbf{L}_{22}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{L}_{11}^{\mathsf{T}}&\mathbf{L}_{21}^{\mathsf{T}}\\
\bm{0}&\mathbf{L}_{22}^{\mathsf{T}}\end{bmatrix},

(2.22)

where
\mathbf{L}_{11}
and
\mathbf{L}_{22}
are lower triangular matrices and
\mathbf{L}_{21}
is a dense matrix.
Performing the matrix multiplication on the right yields a block matrix equation,

\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\\
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{L}_{11}\mathbf{L}_{11}^{\mathsf{T}}&\mathbf{L}_{11}\mathbf{L}_{21}^{\mathsf{T}}\\
\mathbf{L}_{21}\mathbf{L}_{11}^{\mathsf{T}}&\mathbf{L}_{21}\mathbf{L}_{21}^{\mathsf{T}}+\mathbf{L}_{22}\mathbf{L}_{22}^{\mathsf{T}}\end{bmatrix},

(2.23)

from which we can derive a system of three matrix equations,

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}_{11}\mathbf{L}_{11}^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.24)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}_{11}\mathbf{L}_{21}^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.25)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{L}_{21}\mathbf{L}_{21}^{\mathsf{T}}+\mathbf{L}_{22}\mathbf{L}_{22}^{\mathsf{T}}.

(2.26)

Upon inspection of the first equation, we identify
\mathbf{L}_{11}
as the lower triangular Cholesky factor of the train covariance matrix
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
.
Calculating
\mathbf{L}_{11}
takes
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
time, but this cost can be amortised by caching and reusing
\mathbf{L}_{11}
as long as
\mathbf{X}
stays fixed.
Solving the remaining equations for
\mathbf{L}_{21}
and
\mathbf{L}_{22}
results in

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{21}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{L}_{11}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\right)^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.27)

\displaystyle\mathbf{L}_{22}

\displaystyle=\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}-\mathbf{L}_{21}\mathbf{L}_{21}^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.28)

where calculating
\mathbf{L}_{21}
consists of solving
n_{*}
triangular linear systems in
\mathcal{O}(n^{2}n_{*})
time, and
computing
\mathbf{L}_{22}
requires
\mathcal{O}(nn_{*}^{2}+n_{*}^{3})
time to perform some matrix operations and another Cholesky decomposition.
Thus, repeated cubic costs in
n
can be avoided by caching
\mathbf{L}_{11}
.

2.1.3
Covariance Functions

In the previous sections, we assumed the existence of a positive semi-definite covariance function
k
which intuitively captures similarity by expressing the covariance in output space as a function of the input space, that is
k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\Cov(f(\bm{x}),f(\bm{x}^{\prime}))
.
In general, given two valid covariance functions
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, we can combine them to create a new valid covariance function
k_{3}
, for example, using multiplication
k_{3}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=k_{1}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})k_{2}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
or addition
k_{3}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=k_{1}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})+k_{2}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
.
Choosing any particular covariance function will have a strong influence on the properties of the resulting Gaussian process regression model.
For example, some covariance functions are
stationary
, which means that
k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
only depends on
\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}
rather than
\bm{x}
and
\bm{x}^{\prime}
individually.
This property makes stationary covariance functions invariant to translations in the input space.
In the following, we will discuss and illustrate some commonly used covariance functions for the case of
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Squared Exponential

The squared exponential covariance function is arguably the most commonly used covariance function in the context of Gaussian processes.
It is stationary and sometimes also called the Gaussian radial basis function kernel, because it can be expressed as

k_{\mathrm{SE}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime};\ell)=\exp\left(-\frac{\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{2\ell^{2}}\right),

(2.29)

which is proportional to the density of a Gaussian distribution.
In the expression above,
\ell
is a length scale parameter which influences how quickly functions from the Gaussian process will change.
Smaller length scales lead to faster changes and larger length scales produce smoother functions (see
Figure

2.1
for an illustration).

Figure 2.1
:
Samples from a Gaussian process prior using squared exponential covariance function and different length scales
\ell
. Smaller length scales lead to faster changes and larger length scales produce smoother functions.

For multi-dimensional inputs, that is
d>1
, it is common to use a separate length scale per input dimension, which is also known as automatic relevance determination.
It is also common to include a signal scale parameter which multiplies the whole expression to adapt the scale in the output space.
In general, functions from a Gaussian process prior with squared exponential covariance function will be infinitely differentiable.
If somewhat less smooth functions are desired, the next type of covariance function might be more appropriate.

Matérn

The general expression of the Matérn covariance function is given by

k_{\mathrm{Mat}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime};\ell,\nu)=\frac{2^{1-\nu}}{\Gamma(\nu)}\left(\sqrt{2\nu}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}}{\ell}\right)^{\nu}K_{\nu}\left(\sqrt{2\nu}\frac{\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}}{\ell}\right)

(2.30)

where
\ell
is a length scale parameter akin to the one used by the squared exponential covariance function,
\nu
is a smoothness parameter which controls the degree of differentiability of the resulting functions,
\Gamma
is the gamma function which generalises the factorial function, and
K_{\nu}
is the modified Bessel function of the second kind of order
\nu
.

Figure 2.2
:
Samples from a Gaussian process prior with Matérn covariance function using different length scales
\ell
and smoothness
\nu
. Increasing
\nu
leads to smoother functions.

The Matérn covariance function is a good choice to model continuous functions which are somewhat smooth but not infinitely differentiable.
It can also be decorated with an amplitude or signal scale parameter, and its length scale parameter can also be extended to length scales per input dimension if
d>1
.
For specific values of
\nu
, its analytic expression can be substantially simplified.
Commonly used values of
\nu
are
\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
,
\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}
, and
\nicefrac{{5}}{{2}}
, for which

\displaystyle k_{\mathrm{Mat}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime};\ell,\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}})

\displaystyle=\exp\left(-\frac{\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}}{\ell}\right),

(2.31)

\displaystyle k_{\mathrm{Mat}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime};\ell,\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}})

\displaystyle=\left(1{\,+\,}\frac{\sqrt{3}\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}}{\ell}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}}{\ell}\right),

(2.32)

\displaystyle k_{\mathrm{Mat}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime};\ell,\nicefrac{{5}}{{2}})

\displaystyle=\left(1{\,+\,}\frac{\sqrt{5}\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}}{\ell}+\frac{5\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{3\ell^{2}}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{5}\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}}{\ell}\right).

(2.33)

As
\nu
increases, the resulting functions become smoother (see
Figure

2.2
), and in the limit of
\nu\to\infty
, the Matérn covariance function converges to the squared exponential covariance function.
Like the squared exponential, the Matérn covariance function is also stationary.

Figure 2.3
:
Samples from a Gaussian process prior with periodic covariance function using different length scales
\ell
and period lengths
p
. Smaller period lengths
p
result in functions which repeat themselves quicker, while larger
p
increases the interval between repetitions.

Periodic

As the name suggests, the periodic covariance function models periodically repeating functions.
It can be written as

k_{\mathrm{Per}}(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime};\ell,p)=\exp\left(-\frac{2\sin^{2}\left(\frac{\pi}{p}\left\lVert\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime}\right\rVert_{2}\right)}{\ell^{2}}\right),

(2.34)

where
\ell
is a length scale and
p
is the period length after which the function repeats itself (see
Figure

2.3
for some examples).
In this form, the periodic covariance function is strictly periodic, allowing no room for deviations or changes over time, which might not be a realistic assumption for certain real-world settings.
Therefore, it is often combined with a squared exponential or Matérn covariance function.
Multiplying both covariance functions together results in functions which are locally periodic but still allowed to change slightly for different regions in the input space.

2.1.4
Model Selection

Most covariance functions, including the ones discussed in the previous section, require the selection of some parameters, such as length scales or the period length.
In rare circumstances, these parameters can be selected based on ground truth information or expert knowledge.
However, in most circumstances, we would prefer an automatic, rigorous, and data-informed decision.
The gold standard for Gaussian process regression, and arguably Bayesian inference in general, is marginal likelihood optimisation.

In Bayesian inference, the marginal likelihood represents the probability of observing the given training data under a particular model while considering all possible parameter configurations of the given model.
The marginal likelihood is also called the
evidence
, and the procedure of maximising it is often referred to as
type-II maximum likelihood
or
empirical Bayes
.
For a thorough introduction to Bayesian inference and the marginal likelihood in the context of machine learning, we refer to
Bishop 2006
.

For Gaussian process regression, a particular model refers to a particular covariance function and its parameters, all possible parameter configurations refers to all possible latent function values, and considering all possible latent function values refers to calculating the integral

\underbracket{p(\bm{y}\mid\mathbf{X},k,\bm{\vartheta},\sigma^{2})}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{marginal likelihood}}\vphantom{|}}=\int\underbracket{p(\bm{y}\mid\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}},\sigma^{2})}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{likelihood}}\vphantom{|}}\,\underbracket{p(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\mid\mathbf{X},k,\bm{\vartheta})}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{prior}}\vphantom{|}}\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}},

(2.35)

where
\mathbf{X}
and
\bm{y}
are respectively the observed inputs and outputs,
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
are the corresponding latent function values,
\bm{\vartheta}
is a vector which contains all parameters used by the covariance function
k
, and
\sigma^{2}
is the amount of observation noise.
Here, prior refers to the distribution over latent function values under the Gaussian process prior (see
Equation

2.2
) and likelihood refers to the conditional distribution of noisy observations given latent function values (see
Equation

2.3
).
In the context of Gaussian process regression, an analytical solution of this integral exists (see
Equation

2.4
), although similar integrals are often intractable.

In the framework of Bayesian inference, it is desirable to maximise the marginal likelihood because it promotes data fit while penalising overly complex models.
Therefore, the resulting model will, at least in theory, achieve an optimal balance between data fit and complexity.
For the actual optimisation, it is typically preferred to consider the logarithm of the marginal likelihood as a function of all quantities which we want to select while keeping everything else constant.
In the context of Gaussian process regression, the observed data
\mathbf{X}
and
\bm{y}
are considered fixed.
Assuming that the covariance function
k
is also fixed, the logarithm of the marginal likelihood as a function of
\bm{\theta}=\{\bm{\vartheta},\sigma^{2}\}
is given by

\mathcal{L}(\bm{\theta})=\underbracket{-\frac{1}{2}\left(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}\left(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}\right)}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{data fit term}}\vphantom{|}}\underbracket{-\frac{1}{2}\log\det\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{model complexity term}}\vphantom{|}}\underbracket{-\frac{n}{2}\log\left(2\pi\right)}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{constant}}\vphantom{|}},

(2.36)

where the fact that
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
depends on
\bm{\vartheta}
is suppressed to simplify the notation.
Technically, the mean function
\mu
could also depend on further parameters, but, for simplicity, this case is also not considered here.
Typically,
\mathcal{L}
is optimised using gradient-based optimisation, which requires computation of the partial derivatives

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta_{i}}\mathcal{L}(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\left(\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}\left(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}\right)\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\frac{\partial\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)}{\partial\theta_{i}}\left(\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}\left(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\frac{1}{2}\tr\left(\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}\frac{\partial\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)}{\partial\theta_{i}}\right).

(2.37)

Due to the inverse matrices, calculating these partial derivatives tends to be computationally expensive or even intractable without further approximations.

Chapter

5
discusses methodological contributions to make marginal likelihood optimisation in the context of Gaussian process regression more efficient.
An extensive amount of research literature on Gaussian processes introduces methods and techniques to improve the scalability of marginal likelihood optimisation or posterior inference.
Some of these existing approaches, which are particularly relevant for this dissertation, will be discussed in the next section.

2.2
Scalable Inference

In the previous sections, we have established some background about Gaussian processes and discussed relevant computations which are necessary to perform inference.
Unfortunately, in most cases, the time and space requirements of standard inference methods respectively obey cubic and quadratic scaling relative to the amount of training data, which quickly becomes intractable.
The overall goal of this dissertation is to contribute methods for scalable inference in Gaussian processes.
This section provides an overview of existing methods and related work on this topic.
We start by discussing sparse and variational methods, which generally represent a large dataset with a smaller amount of data or inducing points.
Afterwards, we explain random features, which construct unbiased estimators by leveraging the property that covariance functions correspond to implicit inner products.
Subsequently, we cover methods based on structured kernel matrices, which exploit certain linear algebraic structure for efficient computations.
Finally, we conclude with approaches involving iterative linear system solvers, which circumvent explicit matrix inverses or decompositions by performing iterative optimisation to obtain the same result.
Iterative linear system solvers are particularly important for this dissertation because they are a core component of all original contributions throughout this dissertation.

2.2.1
Sparse and Variational Methods

To deal with the
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
and
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
computational time and space complexities of matrix inversion via Cholesky decomposition, researchers developed
sparse
methods which use a set of
m\ll n

inducing points
to represent the observed training data.
While effective at improving inference speed, this approach requires a principled way to select inducing points.

A naive approach to choose inducing points is to select a subset of the data uniformly at random.
Conditioning the Gaussian process on a subset of size
m
reduces the computational complexity to
\mathcal{O}(m^{3})
.
However,
Quiñonero-Candela & Rasmussen 2005
state that they
‘would not generally expect [subset of data] to be a competitive method, since it would seem impossible (even with fairly redundant data and a good choice of the subset) to get a realistic picture of the uncertainties, when only a part of the training data is even considered’
.

The Nyström approximation
(
Williams & Seeger 2000
)
uses a subset
\mathbf{Z}
of
m\ll n
examples from the whole dataset
\mathbf{X}
to approximate
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}
as
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}
.
This approach is motivated by considering the eigendecomposition of a joint matrix
\mathbf{K}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}
, which is assumed to be of rank
m
,

\mathbf{K}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}\\
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}\end{bmatrix}\mathbf{\Lambda}\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}\\
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}\end{bmatrix}^{\mathsf{T}}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}&\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}}\\
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}&\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\\
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\end{bmatrix}.

(2.38)

Using
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}
, we can solve for
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}
, obtaining
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{-1}
.
Finally, we can substitute this expression for
\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}
to calculate
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}
as

\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{-1}\mathbf{U}_{\mathbf{Z}}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}},

(2.39)

obtaining the Nyström approximation.
The equation above requires
\mathbf{K}
to be full rank, but, in practice,
\mathbf{K}
will be low rank if
\mathbf{Z}
is a subset of
\mathbf{X}
.
However, even if
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}
is low rank due to
m\ll n
, the matrix
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
still has full rank because
\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
has full rank.

Quiñonero-Candela & Rasmussen 2005
created a taxonomy of sparse Gaussian processes, distinguishing between the Subset of Regressors (SoR) approximation
(
Silverman 1985
;
Wahba et al. 1999
;
Smola & Bartlett 2001
)
, the Deterministic Training Conditional (DTC) approximation
(
Csató & Opper 2002
;
Seeger et al. 2003
)
, the Fully Independent Training Conditional (FITC) approximation
(
Snelson & Ghahramani 2005
)
and the Partially Independent Training Conditional (PITC) approximation
(
Schwaighofer & Tresp 2002
)
.
Each category in this taxonomy corresponds to a different joint prior distribution over latent function values
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
of the observed data
\mathbf{X}
and unobserved data
\mathbf{X}_{*}
.

With
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}_{*}}
,
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}
and
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}_{*}}
, the joint prior distributions of all approximations share the same mean but differ in their covariance matrices.
In particular,

\displaystyle\Var_{\mathrm{SoR}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}

(2.40)

\displaystyle\Var_{\mathrm{DTC}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}

(2.41)

\displaystyle\Var_{\mathrm{FITC}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}-\mathrm{diag}(\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}})&\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}

(2.42)

\displaystyle\Var_{\mathrm{PITC}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}\right)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}-\mathrm{blockdiag}(\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}})&\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\\
\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix},

(2.43)

where
\mathbf{Q}
corresponds to using a low-rank covariance function and
\mathbf{K}
corresponds to using the true covariance function.

In this framework, the Nyström approximation
(
Williams & Seeger 2000
)
corresponds to

\Var_{\mathrm{Nyst}}\left(\begin{bmatrix}\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\\
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}\right)=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\\
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}}&\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}\end{bmatrix}.

(2.44)

In other words, the Nyström approximation uses the low-rank approximation only for the training data and the true covariance function otherwise.

The methods discussed so far either use heuristics to select specific data examples as inducing points or perform continuous optimisation to select the inducing points by maximising the marginal likelihood of a modified Gaussian process model with approximate prior.
However, the former might be suboptimal and the latter does not guarantee that the modified model will be faithful to the exact model.

Titsias 2009
;
Titsias 2009a
proposed a principled variational framework for sparse Gaussian processes which approximates the exact posterior with a variational posterior.
To this end,
m
inducing points
\mathbf{Z}=\{\bm{z}_{j}\}_{j=1}^{m}
with corresponding latent function values
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{Z}}
are introduced, such that

\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\mid\bm{f}_{\mathbf{Z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\bm{f}_{\mathbf{Z}},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}})\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{f}_{\mathbf{Z}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}),

(2.45)

where we identify
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}
as the Nyström approximation.
Subsequently, a variational distribution
q(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{Z}})
is introduced and a lower bound on the true marginal likelihood,

\log p(\bm{y})\geq\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{q(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{Z}})}\left[\log\frac{p(\bm{y},\bm{f}_{\mathbf{Z}})}{q(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{Z}})}\right]=\mathcal{L}(\mathbf{Z},q),

(2.46)

is formed using Jensen’s inequality.

Titsias 2009
;
Titsias 2009a
derives the optimal variational distribution
q
, such that the lower bound becomes

\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SGPR}}(\mathbf{Z})=\log\mathcal{N}\left(\bm{y}\mid\bm{0},\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)-\frac{1}{2\sigma^{2}}\mathrm{Tr}\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{Q}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\right),

(2.47)

which can be maximised to select the inducing points
\mathbf{Z}
in a principled way.
Furthermore, the corresponding posterior predictive distribution becomes

\displaystyle\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\left(\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}\right),

(2.48)

\displaystyle\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}+\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}})^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y},

(2.49)

\displaystyle\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{X}_{*}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}\mathbf{Z}}\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}-\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}+\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{-1}\right)\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}_{*}},

(2.50)

resulting in an overall time complexity of
\mathcal{O}(nm^{2})
and space complexity of
\mathcal{O}(nm)
.

While a computational complexity of
\mathcal{O}(nm^{2})
with
m\ll n
is a great improvement over
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
, inference can still be intractable if
n
is really large.
Therefore,
Hensman et al. 2013
developed a method to perform
stochastic
variational inference in sparse Gaussian processes which is applicable to larger datasets because optimisation can be performed in mini-batches.

By replacing the
collapsed
variational posterior with an
explicitly parametrised
variational posterior with global mean and covariance parameters
\bm{m}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
and
\mathbf{S}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times m}
,
Hensman et al. 2013
introduce yet another lower bound on the lower bound of
Titsias 2009
;
Titsias 2009a
.
The new lower bound can then be written as

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SVGP}}(\mathbf{Z},\bm{m},\mathbf{S})

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\left(\log\mathcal{N}(y_{i}\mid\mathbf{K}_{\bm{x}_{i}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\bm{m},\sigma^{2})-\frac{c_{i}}{2\sigma^{2}}-\frac{1}{2}\tr\left(\mathbf{S}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{i}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\quad-D_{\mathrm{KL}}\left(\mathcal{N}(\bm{m},\mathbf{S})\mid\mid\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}})\right),

(2.51)

where
\mathbf{K}_{\bm{x}_{i}\mathbf{Z}}
is the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
row of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}
,
c_{i}
is the
i^{\mathrm{th}}
diagonal element of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}
, and
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{i}=\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\bm{x}_{i}}\mathbf{K}_{\bm{x}_{i}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}
.
Importantly, the sum over individual data pairs in
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SVGP}}
enables stochastic gradient methods.
The gradients with respect to
\bm{m}
and
\mathbf{S}
are

\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SVGP}}}{\partial\bm{m}}=\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}-\mathbf{\Lambda}\bm{m}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\frac{\partial\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{SVGP}}}{\partial\mathbf{S}}=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{S}^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{\Lambda},

(2.52)

where
\mathbf{\Lambda}=\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}
.
Furthermore,
Hensman et al. 2013
proposed to use
natural
gradients in the canonical parameters to improve convergence speed.
The natural parameters are
\bm{\theta}_{1}=\mathbf{S}^{-1}\bm{m}
and
\bm{\theta}_{2}=-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{S}^{-1}
and the corresponding natural gradient steps of length
\eta
are

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{1}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=\bm{\theta}_{1}^{(t)}+\eta\left(\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}-\bm{\theta}_{1}^{(t)}\right),

(2.53)

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{2}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=\bm{\theta}_{2}^{(t)}+\eta\left(\bm{\theta}_{2}^{(t)}-\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{\Lambda}\right).

(2.54)

Stochastic approximations of the natural gradients can be obtained using mini-batches, such that each stochastic gradient evaluation has a computational time complexity of
\mathcal{O}(m^{3})
.

More recent work on sparse Gaussian processes include
Wu et al. 2022
, who introduce a prior which only retains correlations for
\kappa
nearest neighbours to produce a sparse kernel matrix, and
Wenger et al. 2022
;
Wenger et al. 2024
, who propose
actions
to create low-rank approximations which guarantee that the approximate predictive variance is always larger than the exact predictive variance.
The additional variance is interpreted as
computational uncertainty
and helps the method achieve well-calibrated predictions while scaling to large datasets.

2.2.2
Random Features

An alternative way to construct low-rank kernel matrix approximations can be derived by considering
Mercer’s theorem

(
Mercer 1909
)
, which informally states that a symmetric positive semi-definite kernel function
k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
can be expressed as an inner product
\left\langle\bm{\varphi}(\bm{x}),\bm{\varphi}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}
in some potentially infinite-dimensional feature space
\mathcal{F}
, where
\bm{\varphi}:\mathcal{X}\to\mathcal{F}
is a feature projection, which maps elements
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
into
\mathcal{F}
.
Random features
(
Rahimi & Recht 2008
)
leverage this property by constructing a particular feature projection
\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{m}
, which depends on random parameters
\bm{\omega}
and maps
\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
into
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, such that the dot product in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
becomes an unbiased approximation of the kernel function,

k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\left\langle\bm{\varphi}(\bm{x}),\bm{\varphi}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\right\rangle_{\mathcal{F}}\approx\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x}^{\prime}).

(2.55)

Random Fourier features
(
Rahimi & Recht 2008
;
Sutherland & Schneider 2015
)
are a prominent example which applies to stationary covariance functions.
To construct random Fourier features,
Rahimi & Recht 2008
leverage
Bochner’s theorem
, which informally states that a covariance function is stationary if and only if it is the Fourier transform of a unique probability measure, leading to the relationship

k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\int p(\bm{\omega})\exp\left(i\bm{\omega}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})\right)\mathop{}\!\mathrm{d}\bm{\omega}=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{\omega}}\left[\exp\left(i\bm{\omega}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{x}-\bm{x}^{\prime})\right)\right].

(2.56)

In this setting,
Rahimi & Recht 2008
show that choosing
\phi_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x})=\sqrt{2}\cos\left(\bm{\omega}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{x}+b\right)
, where
b
is sampled uniformly at random from
[0,2\pi]
, satisfies
k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}_{\bm{\omega}}\left[\phi_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x})\phi_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\right]
.
The variance can be reduced by considering a sample average, which can be expressed as

k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})\approx\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j=1}^{m}\phi_{\bm{\omega}_{j}}(\bm{x})\phi_{\bm{\omega}_{j}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})=\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x}^{\prime}),

(2.57)

where
\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x})
consists of stacking all
\phi_{\bm{\omega}_{j}}(\bm{x})
into a vector and introducing a rescaling factor,

\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x})=\sqrt{\frac{2}{m}}\begin{bmatrix}\cos\left(\bm{\omega}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{x}+b_{1}\right)&\ldots&\cos\left(\bm{\omega}_{m}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{x}+b_{m}\right)\end{bmatrix}^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.58)

where each
\bm{\omega}_{j}
and
b_{j}
are independent and identically distributed.
It is also possible to define

\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\begin{bmatrix}\sin\left(\bm{\omega}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{x}\right)&\cos\left(\bm{\omega}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{x}\right)&\ldots&\sin\left(\bm{\omega}_{m}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{x}\right)&\cos\left(\bm{\omega}_{m}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{x}\right)\end{bmatrix}^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.59)

which does not depend on
b_{j}
and also has lower variance
(
Sutherland & Schneider 2015
)
.

For both variants of
\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x})
, the probability distribution of
\bm{\omega}_{j}
, also known as the
spectral density
, determines which covariance function is approximated.
For example, the squared exponential covariance function
k_{\mathrm{SE}}
corresponds to the multivariate normal distribution and the Matérn covariance function
k_{\mathrm{Mat}}
corresponds to the multivariate Student’s
t
-distribution.

Random features can be used to efficiently draw samples from a Gaussian process.
Suppose we want to draw latent function values
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
at
n
input locations
\mathbf{X}
from a Gaussian process with mean
\mu
and covariance function
k
, such that
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}})
.
We can write
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
as

\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}+\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\bm{w}\qquad\text{with}\qquad\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\mathbf{I}),

(2.60)

where
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times m}
is a matrix which collects
m
random features for each input,

\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{m}}\begin{bmatrix}\phi_{\bm{\omega}_{1}}(\bm{x}_{1})&\dots&\phi_{\bm{\omega}_{m}}(\bm{x}_{1})\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
\phi_{\bm{\omega}_{1}}(\bm{x}_{n})&\dots&\phi_{\bm{\omega}_{m}}(\bm{x}_{n})\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x}_{1})^{\mathsf{T}}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x}_{n})^{\mathsf{T}}\end{bmatrix}.

(2.61)

To verify that
Equation

2.60
yields samples with the desired distribution, we calculate

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}+\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\bm{w}\right]=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}+\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{w}\right]=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}+\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\bm{0}=\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}},

(2.62)

\displaystyle\Var\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Var\left(\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}+\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\bm{w}\right)=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\Var\left(\bm{w}\right)\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{I}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.63)

confirming that
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
has the correct mean
\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}
.
For the variance, we observe that each entry in
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}}
has the form
\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}}(\bm{x}^{\prime})\approx k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})
and thus conclude that
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}}\approx\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
.

Drawing samples in this way has a time complexity of
\mathcal{O}(nm)
, which is considerably faster than the usual
\mathcal{O}(n^{3})
, especially if
m\ll n
.
If we are interested in joint samples
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
at
n
inputs
\mathbf{X}
and
n_{*}
inputs
\mathbf{X}_{*}
, the time complexity becomes
\mathcal{O}((n+n_{*})m)
instead of
\mathcal{O}((n+n_{*})^{3})
.
Furthermore, if
\mathbf{X}
is fixed and
\mathbf{X}_{*}
changes, such that we want to keep
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
but update
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
, expensive conditional sampling, as discussed in
Section

2.1.2
, is not necessary.
Instead, reusing the same random feature parameters
\bm{\omega}
and standard normal sample
\bm{w}
results in samples
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
which are jointly distributed with
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
in
\mathcal{O}(n_{*}m)
instead of
\mathcal{O}(nn_{*}^{2}+n_{*}^{3})
time.
To verify the claim that
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
are jointly distributed, we calculate

\displaystyle\Cov\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}},\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\right)

\displaystyle=\Cov\left(\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}}+\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\bm{w},\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}+\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\bm{w}\right),

(2.64)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{w}\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\right]\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}-\left(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{w}\right]\right)\left(\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{w}\right]\right)^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.65)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}\approx\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}_{*}},

(2.66)

confirming that
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}_{*}}
indeed have the correct covariance.

2.2.3
Structured Kernel Matrices

In certain special cases, kernel matrices will be structured, facilitating more efficient linear algebra operations.
A prominent example are product kernels of the form

k(\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=\prod_{j=1}^{m}k_{j}(\bm{x}_{j},\bm{x}_{j}^{\prime})\quad\text{with}\quad\bm{x}=\left[\bm{x}_{1},\dots,\bm{x}_{m}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad\bm{x}^{\prime}=\left[\bm{x}_{1}^{\prime},\dots,\bm{x}_{m}^{\prime}\right],

(2.67)

where
k_{j}
are individual covariance functions, and
\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime}
are the concatenation of all
\bm{x}_{j},\bm{x}_{j}^{\prime}
respectively.
They are commonly used in multi-task or spatio-temporal settings and induce a kernel matrix
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
which can be factorised as a Kronecker product,

\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{K}_{j}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathbf{K}_{j}=k_{j}(\mathbf{X}_{j},\mathbf{X}_{j})\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbf{X}=\mathbf{X}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathbf{X}_{m},

(2.68)

where the inputs
\mathbf{X}
factorise as a Cartesian product
\mathbf{X}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathbf{X}_{m}
and each Kronecker factor
\mathbf{K}_{j}
consists of the pairwise kernel distances over rows in
\mathbf{X}_{j}
.
To facilitate efficient inference, we can represent the eigendecomposition of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
as

\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{K}_{j}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{j}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{j}\mathbf{Q}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}=\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{j}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{j}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{j}\right)^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.69)

where
\mathbf{Q}_{j}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{j}\mathbf{Q}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}
is the eigendecomposition of Kronecker factor
\mathbf{K}_{j}
with orthogonal matrix
\mathbf{Q}_{j}
and diagonal matrix
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{j}
.
This allows us to compute the inverse of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
as

\displaystyle\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{j}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{j}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{j}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1},

(2.70)

\displaystyle=\left(\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{j}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{j}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{j}\right)^{\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1},

(2.71)

\displaystyle=\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{j}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{j}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{Q}_{j}\right)^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.72)

where
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{j}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
is a diagonal matrix whose inverse is easily computed by taking the reciprocal of each entry on the diagonal
(
Bonilla et al. 2007
;
Stegle et al. 2011
)
.
Importantly, the factorisation implies that the
n\times n
kernel matrix
