\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
factorises as a Kronecker product of
m
factors of size
n_{j}\times n_{j}
and
n=\prod_{j=1}^{m}n_{j}
.
Therefore, computing the eigendecomposition of all Kronecker factors has a total time complexity of
\mathcal{O}(\sum_{j=1}^{m}n_{j}^{3})
compared to
\mathcal{O}(\prod_{j=1}^{m}n_{j}^{3})
without the factorisation, turning multiplicative scaling into additive scaling.
Similarly, the space complexity becomes
\mathcal{O}(\sum_{j=1}^{m}n_{j}^{2})
instead of
\mathcal{O}(\prod_{j=1}^{m}n_{j}^{2})
.

To generate samples, we can express the Cholesky decomposition of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
as

\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{K}_{j}=\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{L}_{j}\mathbf{L}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}=\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{L}_{j}\right)\left(\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{L}_{j}\right)^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.73)

such that
\bigotimes_{j=1}^{m}\mathbf{L}_{j}
can be used in an efficient way.

In addition to the product kernel assumption, the main limitation of this method is that it must be possible to express the observed inputs
\mathbf{X}
as a Cartesian product
\mathbf{X}_{1}\times\dots\times\mathbf{X}_{m}
, because otherwise the joint covariance matrix
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
no longer exhibits Kronecker structure.
In
Chapter

6
, we propose a method which overcomes this limitation.

Wilson & Nickisch 2015
approximate the original kernel matrix
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}
as

\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}\approx\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XZ}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{ZZ}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{ZX}}\approx\mathbf{W}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{ZZ}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{ZZ}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{ZZ}}\mathbf{W}^{\mathsf{T}}=\mathbf{W}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{ZZ}}\mathbf{W}^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.74)

where
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XZ}}\approx\mathbf{W}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{ZZ}}
corresponds to the interpolation of kernel values at inducing points
\mathbf{Z}
using interpolation weights
\mathbf{W}
to compute kernel values for inputs
\mathbf{X}
.
Since
\mathbf{Z}
are inducing points, their locations can be chosen arbitrarily.

Wilson & Nickisch 2015
leverage this property by placing
\mathbf{Z}
on a grid such that
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{ZZ}}
exhibits Kronecker or Toeplitz structure, facilitating fast matrix multiplication which can be exploited by iterative methods to perform inference (see
Section

2.2.4
).
Since iterative methods do not require explicit matrix inverses or decompositions, the number of inducing points used for interpolation can be substantially larger than the number of inducing points used by variational methods.
However, scalability in the number of input dimensions is strongly limited by the fact that the required number of inducing points to create a dense grid increases exponentially with the number of dimensions.

To address these scalability issues with respect to the number of input dimensions,
Gardner et al. 2018a
approximate the original kernel with a Hadamard product of one-dimensional interpolated kernels.
Every interpolated kernel is further approximated as a low-rank Lanczos decomposition,

\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}\approx\mathbf{Q}_{1}\mathbf{T}_{1}\mathbf{Q}_{1}^{\mathsf{T}}\odot\dots\odot\mathbf{Q}_{d}\mathbf{T}_{d}\mathbf{Q}_{d}^{\mathsf{T}},

(2.75)

which gives rise to an algebraic structure that allows for efficient matrix multiplication.
While this addresses the scalability issues, it is usually not competitive with other methods due to the errors introduced by the approximations
(
Kapoor et al. 2021
)
.

2.2.4
Iterative Linear System Solvers

In the context of Gaussian processes, the biggest computational challenge typically consists of evaluating expressions such as
(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\bm{y}
or alike, which feature the inverse of a large positive-definite matrix multiplied with a vector or another matrix.
In general, we can equivalently view such an expression as the solution of a system of linear equations or the minimiser of a convex quadratic objective,

\bm{v}=\mathbf{A}^{-1}\bm{b}\quad\iff\quad\mathbf{A}\bm{v}=\bm{b}\quad\iff\quad\bm{v}=\argmin_{\bm{u}}\;\frac{1}{2}\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\bm{u}-\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{b},

(2.76)

where setting
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
and
\bm{b}=\bm{y}
results in the previously mentioned expression.
The benefits of taking this view become apparent by observing that the matrix
\mathbf{A}
no longer needs to be inverted.
Instead, the solution
\bm{v}
can be computed via iterative gradient-based optimisation of the quadratic objective, because the gradient is simply the residual
\mathbf{A}\bm{u}-\bm{b}
.
Furthermore, the matrix
\mathbf{A}
does not have to be fully instantiated in memory, as long as each entry in
\mathbf{A}
can be evaluated independently.
Therefore, by iterating over the rows of
\mathbf{A}
, the product
\mathbf{A}\bm{u}
can be computed with
\mathcal{O}(n)
space instead of
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
.

The most widely used algorithm to solve these kinds of linear systems or their equivalent quadratic optimisation problems is the method of conjugate gradients
(
Hestenes & Stiefel 1952
)
.
The algorithm leverages the fact that the solution
\bm{v}
can be written as a set of mutually conjugate vectors
\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n}
, where
\bm{p}_{i}
is conjugate to
\bm{p}_{j}
if
\bm{p}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\bm{p}_{j}=0
.
Since these vectors form a basis of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we can express
\bm{v}
as a linear combination
\bm{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\bm{p}_{i}
, which implies that
\mathbf{A}\bm{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\mathbf{A}\bm{p}_{i}
.
For a particular
\bm{p}_{k}
, we thus have

\bm{p}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{b}=\bm{p}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\bm{v}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}\bm{p}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\bm{p}_{i}=c_{k}\bm{p}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\bm{p}_{k}\quad\implies\quad c_{k}=\frac{\bm{p}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{b}}{\bm{p}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\bm{p}_{k}},

(2.77)

where the last equality on the left follows from
\bm{p}_{k}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\bm{p}_{i}=0
for
i\neq k
.
Therefore, we can compute
\bm{v}
by finding a sequence of conjugate vectors
\bm{p}_{1},\dots,\bm{p}_{n}
and calculating the corresponding coefficients
c_{1},\dots,c_{n}
.
The sequence of conjugate vectors is typically computed using an iterative Gram-Schmidt process whose necessary computations can be amortised.
The overall algorithm can be expressed as

\begin{aligned} \bm{v}_{0}&\leftarrow\bm{0}\text{ (typically)},\\
\bm{r}_{0}&\leftarrow\bm{b}-\mathbf{A}\bm{v}_{0},\\
\bm{d}_{0}&\leftarrow\bm{r}_{0},\end{aligned}\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} \alpha_{t}&\leftarrow\frac{\bm{r}_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{r}_{t}}{\bm{d}_{t}\mathbf{A}\bm{d}_{t}},\\
\beta_{t}&\leftarrow\frac{\bm{r}_{t+1}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{r}_{t+1}}{\bm{r}_{t}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{r}_{t}},\end{aligned}\qquad\qquad\begin{aligned} \bm{v}_{t+1}&\leftarrow\bm{v}_{t}+\alpha_{t}\bm{d}_{t},\\
\bm{r}_{t+1}&\leftarrow\bm{r}_{t}-\alpha_{t}\mathbf{A}\bm{d}_{t},\\
\bm{d}_{t+1}&\leftarrow\bm{r}_{t+1}+\beta_{t}\bm{d}_{t}.\end{aligned}

(2.78)

Assuming numerically exact computations, the method of conjugate gradients converges after at most
n
iterations, where the time complexity of each iteration is
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
.
However, the algorithm is typically stopped after a constant number of iterations or when the residual norm
\left\lVert\bm{r}_{t}\right\rVert_{2}
reaches a certain tolerance, and its convergence properties depend on the condition number of
\mathbf{A}
.
In practice, accumulation of rounding errors due to floating point arithmetic can cause problems with keeping track of the current residual and maintaining the property that all
\bm{p}_{j}
are mutually conjugate.
The current residual can be recomputed at the expense of some additional computations, but the latter is difficult to address.
For a more elaborate discussion on the method of conjugate gradients, we refer to
Shewchuk 1994
.

In the context of Gaussian processes,
Gardner et al. 2018
;
Wang et al. 2019
used conjugate gradients to estimate the derivative of the marginal likelihood (see
Section

2.1.4
), which is necessary for model selection.
In particular, the derivative contains two terms which involve an inverse matrix.
The first term is
(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}})
and it is also used when calculating the posterior mean (see
Equation

2.7
).
The second term is a trace term, which is approximated using
Hutchinson’s trace estimator

(
Hutchinson 1990
)
,

\tr\left(\mathbf{H}_{\bm{\theta}}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{H}_{\bm{\theta}}}{\partial\theta_{i}}\right)=\tr\left(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{z}\bm{z}^{\mathsf{T}}\right]\mathbf{H}_{\bm{\theta}}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{H}_{\bm{\theta}}}{\partial\theta_{i}}\right)=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{z}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{H}_{\bm{\theta}}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{H}_{\bm{\theta}}}{\partial\theta_{i}}\bm{z}\right]\approx\frac{1}{s}\sum_{j=1}^{s}\bm{z}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{H}_{\bm{\theta}}^{-1}\frac{\partial\mathbf{H}_{\bm{\theta}}}{\partial\theta_{i}}\bm{z}_{j},

(2.79)

where
\mathbf{H}_{\bm{\theta}}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
,
\bm{\theta}
refers to
\sigma^{2}
and kernel hyperparameters
\bm{\vartheta}
, and
\bm{z}_{j}
are random probe vectors for which
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{z}_{j}\bm{z}_{j}^{\mathsf{T}}\right]=\mathbf{I}
.
Both terms can be addressed at the same time by solving a batch of linear systems,

(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})\;\left[\bm{v}_{\bm{y}},\bm{v}_{1},\dots,\bm{v}_{s}\right]=\left[\bm{y}-\bm{\mu}_{\mathbf{X}},\bm{z}_{1},\dots,\bm{z}_{s}\right],

(2.80)

using the method of conjugate gradients.
Since these linear systems share the same coefficient matrix
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}
, efficient matrix multiplication is applicable.
To calculate the posterior covariance matrix or to generate posterior samples,
Wang et al. 2019
rely on approximate kernel matrix decompositions using the Lanczos algorithm
(
Pleiss et al. 2018
)
.

Wilson et al. 2020
;
Wilson et al. 2021
used conjugate gradients to compute
(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}))
to generate a posterior sample
via pathwise conditioning (see
Equation

2.12
).
As discussed in
Section

2.1.2
, the result can be cached and reused when evaluating the posterior sample at another location.
Therefore, using an iterative linear system solver to compute this expensive term once is a powerful concept which allows Gaussian processes to scale to large amounts of training data.
In the following chapters, all original contributions leverage this powerful combination of iterative linear system solvers and pathwise conditioning in various ways.

Chapter 3
Stochastic Gradient Descent

for Gaussian Processes

After reviewing Gaussian processes and some of the existing literature on scalable inference, this chapter introduces the first methodological contribution of this dissertation.
In particular, we introduce a novel iterative linear system solver for Gaussian processes, namely stochastic gradient descent, which facilitates approximate inference with asymptotically linear time and memory requirements.
Additionally, we develop low-variance optimisation objectives and extend these to inducing points for even greater scalability.
Furthermore, we observe that stochastic gradient descent often produces accurate predictions, even in cases where it does not quickly converge to the optimum.
We explain this through a spectral characterisation of its implicit bias, showing that our algorithm produces predictive distributions close to the true posterior both in regions with sufficient data coverage, and in regions sufficiently far away from the data.
Furthermore, we demonstrate empirically that stochastic gradient descent achieves state-of-the-art performance on sufficiently large-scale or ill-conditioned regression tasks, and a large-scale Bayesian optimisation problem.

This chapter includes content which is adapted from the following publication:

•

J. A. Lin, J. Antorán, S. Padhy, D. Janz, J. M. Hernández-Lobato, and A. Terenin. Sampling from Gaussian Process Posteriors using Stochastic Gradient Descent. In
Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems
, 2023.

My contributions to this project consist of developing major parts of the software implementation, conducting a large amount of experiments, and contributing to writing the manuscript and some of the theoretical results.

3.1
Introduction

Gaussian processes (GPs) provide a comprehensive framework for learning unknown functions in an uncertainty-aware manner.
This often makes Gaussian processes the model of choice for sequential decision-making, achieving state-of-the-art performance in tasks such as optimising molecules in computational chemistry settings
(
Gómez-Bombarelli et al. 2018
)
and automated hyperparameter tuning
(
Snoek et al. 2012
;
Hernández-Lobato et al. 2014
)
.

The main limitation of Gaussian processes is that their computational cost is cubic in the size of the training dataset.
Significant research efforts have been directed at addressing this limitation, resulting in two key classes of scalable inference methods: (i)
inducing point
methods
(
Titsias 2009
;
Hensman et al. 2013
)
, which approximate the GP posterior, and (ii)
conjugate gradient
methods
(
Gardner et al. 2018
;
Wang et al. 2019
)
, which approximate the computation needed to obtain the GP posterior.
Note that in structured settings, such as geospatial learning in low dimensions, specialised techniques are available
(
Wilson & Nickisch 2015
;
Gardner et al. 2018a
;
Wilkinson et al. 2020
)
.
Throughout this work, we focus on the generic setting, where scalability limitations remain unresolved.

In recent years, stochastic gradient descent (SGD) has emerged as the leading technique for training machine learning models at scale, in both deep learning
(
Tian et al. 2023
)
and in related settings such as kernel methods
(
Dai et al. 2014
)
and Bayesian modelling
(
Mandt et al. 2017
)
.
While the principles behind the effectiveness of SGD are not yet fully understood, empirically, SGD often leads to good predictive performance, even when it does not fully converge.
The latter is the default regime in deep learning, and has motivated researchers to study
implicit biases
and related properties of SGD
(
Belkin et al. 2019
;
Zou et al. 2021
)
.

In the context of GPs, SGD is commonly used to learn kernel hyperparameters by optimising the marginal likelihood
(
Cutajar et al. 2016
;
Gardner et al. 2018
;
Chen et al. 2020
;
Chen et al. 2022
)
or closely related variational objectives
(
Titsias 2009
;
Hensman et al. 2013
)
.
In this work, we explore applying SGD to the complementary problem of approximating GP posterior samples given fixed kernel hyperparameters.
In one of his seminal books on statistical learning theory,
Vapnik 1995
famously said:
“When solving a given problem, try to avoid solving a more general problem as an intermediate step.”

Motivated by this viewpoint, as well as the aforementioned property of good performance often not requiring full convergence when using SGD, we ask:
Do the linear systems arising in GP computations necessarily need to be solved to a small error tolerance? If not, can SGD help accelerate these computations?

We answer the latter question affirmatively, with specific contributions as follows:

•

We develop a scheme for drawing GP posterior samples by applying SGD to a quadratic problem.
In particular, we recast the pathwise conditioning technique of
Wilson et al. 2020
;
Wilson et al. 2021
as an optimisation problem to which we apply the low-variance SGD sampling estimator of
Antorán et al. 2023
. Further, we extend the proposed method to inducing point Gaussian processes.

•

We characterise the implicit bias in SGD-approximated GP posteriors showing that despite optimisation not fully converging, these match the true posterior in regions both near to and far from the data.

•

Experimentally, we show that SGD produces strong results, compared to variational and conjugate gradient methods, on both large-scale and ill-conditioned regression tasks, and on a parallel Thompson sampling task, where error bar calibration is paramount.

3.2
Posterior Samples via Stochastic Optimisation

Assuming a zero-mean prior, we now develop and analyse techniques for drawing samples from GP posteriors using SGD by rewriting the pathwise conditioning formula (
2.12
) in terms of two stochastic optimisation problems.
As a preview,
Figure

3.1
illustrates SGD on a pair of toy problems, designed to capture complementary computational difficulties.

3.2.1
Computing the Posterior Mean

We begin by deriving a stochastic objective for the posterior mean.
In particular, the GP posterior mean
\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}
can be expressed in terms of the quadratic optimisation problem

\displaystyle\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{X}}\bm{v}^{*}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})v_{i}^{*},

(3.1)

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{*}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\;\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{y}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}}^{2},

(3.2)

where
\bm{v}^{*}=(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\bm{y}
.
We say that
k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})
are the
canonical basis functions
,
v_{i}
are the
representer weights

(
Schölkopf et al. 2001
)
, and that
\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}}^{2}=\bm{v}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{v}
is the
regulariser
.
The respective optimisation problem for obtaining posterior samples is similar, but involves a stochastic objective, which will be given and analysed in
Section

3.2.2
.

Figure 3.1
:
Comparison of SGD, CG, and SVGP for GP inference with a squared exponential kernel on
10
k data points from
\sin(2x)+\cos(5x)
with observation noise scale
0.5
. We draw
2000
function samples with all methods by running them for
10
minutes on an RTX
2070
GPU.

Infill asymptotics
considers
x_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
. A large number of points near zero result in a very ill-conditioned kernel matrix, preventing CG from converging. SGD converges in all of input space except at the edges of the data. SVGP can summarise the data with only 20 inducing points.

Large domain asymptotics
considers data on a regular grid with fixed spacing.
This problem is better conditioned, allowing SGD and CG to recover the exact solution. However, 1024 inducing points are not enough for SVGP to summarise the data.

The optimisation problem (
3.2
) with optimal representer weight solution
\bm{v}^{*}

requires
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
operations to compute both its square error and regulariser terms exactly.
The square error loss term is amenable to mini-batching, which gives an unbiased estimate in
\mathcal{O}(n)
operations.
Assuming that
k
is stationary, we can stochastically estimate the regulariser with random Fourier features
(
Rahimi & Recht 2008
)
using the approximation
\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}}^{2}\approx\bm{v}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{v}
, where
\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}=\left[\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}_{1}}(\mathbf{X}),\dots,\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}_{q}}(\mathbf{X})\right]
is a random feature matrix such that
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}\approx\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{\Phi}_{\mathbf{X}}^{\mathsf{T}}
(see
Section

2.2.2
for details).
Combining both estimators gives our SGD objective

\mathcal{L}(\bm{v})=\frac{n}{2p}\sum_{i=1}^{p}(y_{i}-\mathbf{K}_{\bm{x}_{i}\mathbf{X}}\bm{v})^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\sum_{j=1}^{q}(\bm{\phi}_{\bm{\omega}_{j}}(\mathbf{X})^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{v})^{2}

(3.3)

where
p
is the mini-batch size and
q
is the number of random features.
The regulariser term is unbiased even when drawing a single random feature because the number of features only controls the variance.

Equation

3.3
presents
\mathcal{O}(n)
time complexity, in contrast to
\mathcal{O}(n^{2})
for exact evaluation.

3.2.2
Computing Posterior Samples

We now frame GP posterior samples in a manner amenable to SGD computation similarly to
Equation

3.3
.
First, we rewrite the pathwise conditioning expression (
2.12
) as

f_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}=\underbracket{f_{(\cdot)}\vphantom{\bm{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{prior}}\vphantom{|}}+\underbracket{\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{X}}\bm{v}^{*}\vphantom{\bm{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{posterior mean}}\vphantom{|}}-\underbracket{\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{XX}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon})\vphantom{\bm{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}}}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{\text{uncertainty reduction term}}\vphantom{|}},

(3.4)

where
\bm{\varepsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})
,
f\sim\mathcal{GP}(0,k)
, and
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
is
f
evaluated at
\mathbf{X}
.
We approximate the prior function sample using random Fourier features (see
Section

2.2.2
) and the posterior mean with the minimiser
\bm{v}^{*}
of
Equation

3.3
obtained by SGD.
Each posterior sample’s uncertainty reduction term is parametrised by a set of representer weights given by a linear solve against a noisy prior sample evaluated at the observed inputs, namely
\bm{\alpha}^{*}=(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon})
.
We construct an optimisation objective targeting a sample’s optimal representer weights as

\bm{\alpha}^{*}=\argmin_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\;\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}}^{2},\quad\begin{aligned} \bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}&\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}),\\
\bm{\varepsilon}&\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}).\end{aligned}

(3.5)

Applying mini-batch estimation to this objective results in high gradient variance, since the presence of
\varepsilon_{i}
makes the targets noisy.
To avoid this, we modify
Equation

3.5
as

\bm{\alpha}^{*}=\argmin_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}}\;\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{\alpha}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}}^{2},\quad\begin{aligned} \bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}&\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}),\\
\bm{\delta}&\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{I}),\end{aligned}

(3.6)

moving the noise term into the regulariser.
This modification preserves the optimal representer weights since both objectives have the same gradient and are thus equal up to a constant.

Proof.

Let
\bm{w}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\mathbf{I})
, such that
\bm{\varepsilon}=\sigma\bm{w}
and
\bm{\delta}=\sigma^{-1}\bm{w}
.

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\alpha}}\;\eqref{eqn:samples-optim}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{\alpha}}\left[{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\smash{\textstyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}}\left\lVert\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}}^{2}\right]

(3.7)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\sigma\bm{w}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{\alpha}\right)+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{\alpha}

(3.8)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{\alpha}+\sigma\bm{w}-\sigma^{2}\bm{\alpha}\right)

(3.9)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\alpha}}\;\eqref{eqn:samples-optim-reduced}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{\alpha}}\left[{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\smash{\textstyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}}\left\lVert\bm{\alpha}-\bm{\delta}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}}^{2}\right]

(3.10)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{\alpha}\right)+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\left(\bm{\alpha}-\sigma^{-1}\bm{w}\right)

(3.11)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{\alpha}+\sigma\bm{w}-\sigma^{2}\bm{\alpha}\right)

(3.12)

The claim follows from the fact that these two expressions are identical.
∎

This generalises the variance reduction technique of
Antorán et al. 2023
to the GP setting.

Figure

3.2
illustrates mini-batch gradient variance for these objectives.
Applying the mini-batch and random feature estimators of
Equation

3.3
, each evaluation of the objective takes
\mathcal{O}(ns)
time, where
s
is the number of posterior samples.

Figure 3.2
:
Left: gradient variance throughout optimisation for a single-sample mini-batch estimator of
Equation

3.5
(Loss
1
), and the proposed sampling objective from
Equation

3.6
(Loss
2
), on the
elevators
dataset (
n\approx 16
k). Middle: RMSE and negative log-likelihood (NLL) obtained by SGD and its inducing point variants on the
houseelectric
dataset (
n\approx 2
M) for different numbers of inducing points. Right: total runtimes on an A100 GPU.

3.2.3
Inducing Points

So far, our sampling objectives have presented linear cost in dataset size.
In the large-scale setting, algorithms with costs independent of the dataset size are often preferable, which can be achieved through
inducing point posteriors

(
Titsias 2009
;
Hensman et al. 2013
)
.

Let
\mathbf{Z}
be a set of
m
inducing points.
Applying pathwise conditioning to the inducing point approximation of
Titsias 2009
(see
Section

2.2.1
for details) gives the expression

f_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}=f_{(\cdot)}+\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}(\bm{y}-(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}+\bm{\varepsilon})),

(3.13)

where
f\sim\mathcal{GP}(0,k)
is a prior function sample,
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\bm{f}_{\mathbf{Z}}
, and
\bm{\varepsilon}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})
.

Proof.

Let
\mathbf{A}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}
.

\displaystyle\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[f_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}\right]

\displaystyle=\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[f_{(\cdot)}\right]+\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{A}(\bm{y}-(\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}[\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}]+\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}\left[\bm{\varepsilon}\right]))

(3.14)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}+\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}})^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}

(3.15)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\bm{v}^{*}=\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}\qquad\text{(see \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:posterior_mean_titsias})}

(3.16)

\displaystyle\Var\left(f_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}\right)

\displaystyle=\Var\left(f_{(\cdot)}\right)+\Var\left(\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{A}(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}+\bm{\varepsilon})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\Cov\left(f_{(\cdot)},\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{A}(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}+\bm{\varepsilon})\right)

\displaystyle\quad-\Cov\left(\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{A}(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}+\bm{\varepsilon}),f_{(\cdot)}\right)

(3.17)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)(\cdot)}+\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{A}\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)\mathbf{A}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}(\cdot)}

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{A}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}(\cdot)}-\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}(\cdot)}

(3.18)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)(\cdot)}-\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{A}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}(\cdot)}\quad\text{(\emph{Woodbury identity})}

(3.19)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)(\cdot)}-\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}-\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}+\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{-1}\right)\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}(\cdot)}

(3.20)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}\qquad\text{(see \lx@cref{creftypecap\lx@tilde refnum}{eq:posterior_cov_titsias})}

(3.21)

This confirms the claim that
f_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}
has the desired expected value and variance.
∎

Following
Wild et al. 2023
, the optimal inducing point posterior mean
\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}
can therefore be written as

\displaystyle\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\bm{v}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}k(\cdot,\bm{z}_{j})v_{j}^{*},

(3.22)

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{*}

\displaystyle=\argmin_{\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\;\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{y}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\bm{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}}^{2},

(3.23)

and we can again parametrise the uncertainty reduction term as
\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}\bm{\alpha}^{*}
with

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}^{*}=\argmin_{\bm{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}}\;\frac{1}{2}\|\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}+\bm{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\bm{\alpha}\|_{2}^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}}^{2},\quad\begin{aligned} \bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}&\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}),\\
\bm{\varepsilon}&\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}).\end{aligned}

(3.24)

Proof.

We can show that these optimisation objectives result in the desired
\bm{v}^{*}
and
\bm{\alpha}^{*}
by deriving their minimisers analytically.
To this end, we first calculate their gradients.

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{v}}\;\eqref{eqn:inducing-optim}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{v}}\Big[{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\left\lVert\bm{y}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\bm{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+{\textstyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}}^{2}\Big]

(3.25)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\left(\bm{y}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\bm{v}\right)+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}\bm{v}

(3.26)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\alpha}}\;\eqref{eqn:inducing-samples-ht-optim}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{\bm{\alpha}}\Big[{\textstyle\frac{1}{2}}\|\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}+\bm{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\bm{\alpha}\|_{2}^{2}+{\textstyle\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}}\left\lVert\bm{\alpha}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}}^{2}\Big]

(3.27)

\displaystyle=-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}+\bm{\varepsilon}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\bm{\alpha}\right)+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}\bm{\alpha}

(3.28)

Settings these gradients to zero and solving for
\bm{v}
and
\bm{\alpha}
yields
\bm{v}^{*}
and
\bm{\alpha}^{*}
.

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}

(3.29)

\displaystyle\bm{\alpha}^{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\left(\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}+\bm{\varepsilon}\right)

(3.30)

We can rearrange
\bm{v}^{*}
and
\bm{\alpha}^{*}
by applying the Woodbury identity twice.

\displaystyle\bm{v}^{*}

\displaystyle=\left(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}

(3.31)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{-2}\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}+\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}

(3.32)

\displaystyle=\left[\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\left(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\right]\sigma^{-2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}

(3.33)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\left[\mathbf{I}-\left(\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}+\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\right)^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\right]\sigma^{-2}\bm{y}

(3.34)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}\left(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I}\right)^{-1}\bm{y}

(3.35)

The case for
\bm{\alpha}^{*}
is analogous.
Therefore,
Equation

3.13
can be expressed as

f_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}=f_{(\cdot)}+\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{Z}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\bm{\alpha}^{*}),

(3.36)

which demonstrates that optimising the objectives from
Equations

3.23
and
3.24
results in the desired
\bm{v}^{*}
and
\bm{\alpha}^{*}
to draw samples from the inducing point posterior.
∎

Exact implementation of
Equation

3.24
is precluded by the need to sample
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}
from a Gaussian with covariance
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{Z}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{Z}}^{-1}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{Z}\mathbf{X}}
.
However, we identify this matrix as a Nyström approximation to
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}
.
Thus, we can approximate
Equation

3.24
by replacing
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}
with
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
, which can be sampled with random Fourier features (see
Section

2.2.2
).
The approximation error is small when
m
is large and the inducing points are close enough to the data, which is effectively required for inducing point methods to perform well in general.
Therefore, we use
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}
instead of
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}^{[\mathbf{Z}]}
and apply the stochastic estimator from
Equation

3.3
to the inducing point sampling objective.

The inducing point objectives differ from those presented in previous sections in that there are
\mathcal{O}(m)
instead of
\mathcal{O}(n)
learnable representer weights
\bm{v}
and
\bm{\alpha}
, and we may choose the value of
m
and locations
\mathbf{Z}
freely.
The cost of inducing point representer weight updates is thus
\mathcal{O}(ms)
, where
s
is the number of samples.
This contrasts with the
\mathcal{O}(m^{3})
update cost of stochastic gradient variational Gaussian processes
(
Hensman et al. 2013
)
.

Figure

3.2
shows that SGD with
m\approx 100
k inducing points matches the performance of regular SGD on
houseelectric
with
n\approx 2
M, but is an order of magnitude faster.

Figure 3.3
:

Convergence of GP posterior mean with SGD and CG as a function of time on the
elevators
dataset (
n\approx 16
k) while setting the noise scale to (i) maximise exact GP marginal likelihood and (ii) to
0.001
, labelled
low noise
. We plot from left to right: test RMSE, RMSE to the exact GP mean at the test inputs, representer weight error
\left\lVert\bm{v}-\bm{v}^{*}\right\rVert_{2}
, and RKHS error
\left\lVert\bm{v}-\bm{v}^{*}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}}
. In the latter two plots, the low noise setting is shown on the bottom.

3.2.4
Implicit Bias and Posterior Geometry

We have detailed an SGD-based scheme for obtaining approximate samples from a posterior Gaussian process.
Despite SGD’s significantly lower cost per iteration than CG, its convergence to the true optima, shown in
Figure

3.3
, is much slower with respect to both Euclidean representer weight space, and the reproducing kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) induced by the kernel.
Nonetheless, the predictions obtained by SGD are very close to those of the exact GP, and effectively achieve the same test RMSE.
Moreover,
Figure

3.4
shows the SGD posterior on a 1D toy task exhibits error bars of the correct width close to the data, and which revert smoothly to the prior far away from the data.
Empirically, differences between the SGD and exact posteriors concentrate at the borders of data-dense regions.

We now argue the behaviour seen in
Figure

3.4
is a general feature of SGD: one can expect it to obtain good performance even in situations where it does not converge to the exact solution.
Consider posterior function samples in pathwise form, namely
f_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}=f_{(\cdot)}+\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{X}}\bm{v}
, where
f\sim\mathcal{GP}(0,k)
is a prior function sample and
\bm{v}
are the learnable representer weights.
We characterise the behaviour of SGD-computed approximate posteriors by splitting the input space
\mathcal{X}
into three regions, which we call the
prior
,
interpolation
, and
extrapolation
regions.
The prior region consists of points which are sufficiently distant from the observed training data, the interpolation region refers to points which are close to the observed training data, and the extrapolation region corresponds to the remaining space after eliminating the prior and interpolation regions.
In the following, we will discuss and theoretically analyse these regions separate from each other.

The Prior Region

This corresponds to points sufficiently distant from the observed data.
Here, for kernels that decay over space, the canonical basis functions
k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})
go to zero.
Thus, both the true posterior and any approximations formulated pathwise revert to the prior.
More precisely, let
\mathcal{X}=\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
k
satisfy
\lim_{c\to\infty}k(c\,\bm{x},\bm{x}^{\prime})=0
for all
\bm{x}^{\prime}
and
\bm{x}
in
\mathcal{X}
, and let
f_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}
be given by
f_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}=f_{(\cdot)}+\mathbf{K}_{(\cdot)\mathbf{X}}\bm{v}
, with
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
. Then, by passing the limit through the sum, for any fixed
\bm{v}
, it follows immediately that
\lim_{c\to\infty}f_{c\,\bm{x}\mid\bm{y}}=f_{c\,\bm{x}}
. Therefore, SGD cannot incur error in regions which are sufficiently far away from the data.
This effect is depicted in
Figure

3.4
.

The Interpolation Region

This includes points close to the training data.
We characterise this region via subspaces of the RKHS, where we show that SGD incurs small error.

Let
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}
be the eigendecomposition of the kernel matrix.
We index the eigenvalues
\mathbf{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dotsc,\lambda_{n})
in descending order.
Define the
spectral basis functions
as eigenvalue-weighted and eigenvector-rotated linear combinations of canonical basis functions

u^{(i)}(\cdot)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{U_{ji}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{j}).

(3.37)

These functions, which also appear in kernel principal component analysis, are orthonormal with respect to the RKHS inner product.
To characterise them further, we lift the Courant-Fischer characterisation of eigenvalues and eigenvectors to the RKHS
\mathcal{H}_{k}
induced by
k
, obtaining the expression

u^{(i)}(\cdot)=\argmax_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{k}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}u(\bm{x}_{i})^{2}:\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=1,\left\langle u,u^{(j)}\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=0,\forall j<i\right\}.

(3.38)

This means in particular that the top spectral basis function
u^{(1)}
is a function of fixed RKHS norm, that is, of fixed degree of smoothness, as defined by the kernel
k
, which takes maximal values at the observations
\bm{x}_{1},...,\bm{x}_{n}
.
Thus,
u^{(1)}
will be large near clusters of observations.
The same will be true for the subsequent spectral basis functions, which also take maximal values at the observations, but are constrained to be RKHS-orthogonal to previous spectral basis functions.
The proof is provided in Appendix
A.1.3
.

Figure

3.4
confirms that the top spectral basis functions are indeed centred on the observed data.

Figure 3.4
:
SGD error and spectral basis functions. Top-left: SGD (blue) and exact GP (black, dashed) fit to a toy regression dataset (
n=10
k). Top-right: Wasserstein-2 distance (W2) between both processes’ marginals. The Wasserstein-2 distances are low near the data (interpolation region) and far away from the training data. The error concentrates at the edges of the data (extrapolation region). Bottom: The low-index spectral basis functions lie on the interpolation region, where the Wasserstein-2 distances are low, while functions of index
10
and larger lie on the extrapolation region where the error is large.

Empirically, SGD matches the true posterior in this region.
We formalise this observation by showing that SGD converges quickly in the directions spanned by spectral basis functions with large eigenvalues.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
\delta>0
and
\sigma^{2}>0
.
Let
\proj_{u^{(i)}}(\cdot)
be the orthogonal projection onto the subspace spanned by
u^{(i)}
.
Let
\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}
be the exact posterior predictive mean, and let
\hat{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}
be the posterior predictive mean obtained by Polyak-averaged SGD after
t
steps, starting from an initial set of representer weights equal to zero, and using a sufficiently small learning rate of
0<\eta<\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1}+\sigma^{2})}
.
Assume the stochastic estimate of the gradient is
G
-sub-Gaussian.
Then, with probability
1-\delta
, we have for
i=1,...,n
that

\big\lVert\proj_{u^{(i)}}\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}-\proj_{u^{(i)}}\hat{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}\big\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}\leq\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}^{3}}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\sigma^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{n}{\delta}}\right).

(3.39)

We expect
G
to be at most
\mathcal{O}(\lambda_{1}^{2}\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{\infty})
with high probability.
The proof for
Proposition

3.1
, an additional pointwise convergence bound, and a variant which handles projections onto general subspaces spanned by basis functions are provided in Appendix
A.1
.

The result extends immediately from the posterior mean to posterior samples by replacing
\bm{y}
with
\bm{f}_{\mathbf{X}}+\bm{\varepsilon}
.
Therefore,
SGD quickly converges to the posterior GP in the data-dense region
, where the spectral basis functions corresponding to large eigenvalues are located.
Since convergence speed on the span of each basis function is independent of the magnitude of the other basis functions’ eigenvalues, SGD can perform well even when the kernel matrix is ill-conditioned.
This is shown in
Figure

3.3
.

The Extrapolation Region

This can be found by elimination from the input space of the prior and interpolation regions, in both of which SGD incurs low error.
Consider the spectral basis functions
u^{(i)}(\cdot)
with small eigenvalues.
By orthogonality of
u^{(1)},...,u^{(n)}
, such functions cannot be large near the observations while retaining a prescribed norm.
Their mass is therefore placed away from the observations.
SGD converges slowly in this region, resulting in a large error in its solution in both a Euclidean and RKHS sense, as seen in
Figure

3.3
.
Fortunately, due to the lack of data in the extrapolation region, the excess test error incurred due to SGD non-convergence may be low, resulting in
benign non-convergence

(
Zou et al. 2021
)
.
Similar phenomena have been observed in the inverse problems literature, where this is called
iterative regularisation

(
Hansen 1998
;
Jin & Kereta 2023
)
.

Figure

3.4
shows that the Wasserstein-2 distance to the exact GP predictions is large in this region, as, when initialised at zero, SGD tends to return small representer weights, thus reverting to the prior.

3.3
Experiments

We now turn to empirical evaluation of SGD GPs, focusing on their predictive and decision-making properties.
We compare SGD GPs with the two most popular scalable GP techniques: preconditioned conjugate gradient (CG)
(
Wang et al. 2019
)
and sparse stochastic variational inference (SVGP)
(
Hensman et al. 2013
)
.
For all methods, we use
2000
random Fourier features to draw each prior function used for computing posterior function samples via pathwise conditioning.
Following
Wang et al. 2019
, we use a pivoted Cholesky preconditioner of size
100
for CG, except in cases where this slows down convergence, where we report results without preconditioning instead.
For SVGP, we use
m=4096
inducing points for all datasets, initialising their locations with the
k
-means algorithm.
In all SGD experiments, we use a Nesterov momentum value of
0.9
and Polyak averaging
(
Antorán et al. 2023
)
.
Additionally, we draw
100
new random features at each step to estimate the regulariser term.
Furthermore, we perform gradient clipping using a maximum gradient norm of
0.1
.

3.3.1
Regression Baselines

We first compare SGD-based predictions with baselines in terms of predictive performance, scaling of computational cost with problem size, and robustness to the ill-conditioning of kernel matrices and corresponding linear systems.
Following
Wang et al. 2019
, we consider 9 datasets from the UCI repository
(
Dua & Graff 2017
)
ranging in size from
n=15
k to
n\approx 2
M data points and dimensionality from
d=3
to
d=90
.
We report mean and standard deviation over five
90\%
-train
10\%
-test splits for the small and medium datasets, and three splits for the largest dataset.
For all methods, we use a
Matérn-
\nicefrac{{3}}{{2}}
kernel with a fixed set of hyperparameters obtained using maximum marginal likelihood.
For each dataset, we select a homoscedastic Gaussian noise scale, signal scale and a separate length scale per input.
For datasets with less than
50
k observations, we tune these hyperparameters to maximise the exact GP marginal likelihood.
The cubic cost of this procedure makes it intractable at a larger scale.
Thus, for datasets with more than
50
k observations, we use the following procedure:

1.

From the training data, select a centroid data point uniformly at random.

2.

Select a subset of
10
k data points with the smallest Euclidean distance to the centroid.

3.

Find hyperparameters by maximising the exact GP marginal likelihood on this subset.

4.

Repeat the preceding steps with
10
different centroids and average the hyperparameters.

This approach avoids aliasing bias due to data subsampling and is tractable for large datasets.

We run SGD for
100
k steps using a learning rate of
0.5
to estimate the representer weights of the mean function, a learning rate of
0.1
to draw samples, and a fixed batch size of
512
for both the mean function and samples.
For CG, we run up to
1000
steps for datasets with
n\leq 500
k, and a relative residual norm tolerance of
0.01
.
On the four largest datasets, CG’s cost per step is too large to run
1000
steps.
Instead, we run
100
steps, which takes roughly 9 hours per function sample on a TPUv2 core.
For SVGP, we learn the variational parameters for
m=4096
inducing points by maximising the ELBO with Adam until convergence.
For all methods, we estimate predictive variances for log-likelihood computations from 64 function samples drawn using pathwise conditioning.

Figure 3.5
:
Test RMSE and NLL as a function of compute time for CG and SGD. Step-like discontinuities are due to the fact that the metrics are evaluated at discrete intervals.

Predictive Performance and Scalability with Training Dataset Size

Our complete set of results is provided in
Table

3.1
, including test RMSE, test negative log-likelihood (NLL) and compute time needed to obtain the predictive mean on a single core of a TPUv2 device.
Drawing multiple samples requires repeating this computation, which we perform in parallel.
In the small setting (
n\leq 20
k), taking
100
k steps of SGD presents a compute cost comparable to running CG to tolerance, which usually takes around
500
-
800
steps.
Here, CG eventually converges to the exact solution, while SGD tends to present increased test error due to non-convergence.
In the large setting (
n\geq 100
k), where neither method converges within the provided compute budget, SGD achieves better RMSE and NLL.
SVGP always converges faster than CG and SGD, but it only performs best on the
buzz
dataset, which can likely be summarised well by
m=4096
inducing points.

From
Figure

3.5
, we see that SGD makes the vast majority of its progress in prediction space in its first few iterations, improving roughly monotonically with the number of steps.
Thus, early stopping after
100
k iterations incurs only moderate errors.
In contrast, CG’s initial steps actually increase test error, resulting in very poor performance if stopped too early.
This interacts poorly with the number of CG steps needed and the cost per step, which generally grow with increased amounts of data
(
Terenin et al. 2023
)
.
