Table

6.2
reports the predictive performances and total runtimes of temperature and precipitation prediction.
On this large dataset with millions of examples, LKGP clearly outperforms the sparse baseline methods in both root-mean-square-error and negative log-likelihood.
This is unsurprising, given that LKGP performs inference in the exact GP model while the other methods are subject to a limited number of inducing points, nearest neighbours, or sparse actions.
However, due to latent Kronecker structure, LKGP also requires the least amount of time by a large margin.
Interestingly, VNNGP consistently outperforms SVGP and CaGP on this problem.
We suspect that this is due to the nearest neighbour mechanism working well on these datasets with actual spatial dimensions.

6.4
Discussion

In this chapter, we proposed a highly scalable exact Gaussian process model for product kernels, which leverages latent Kronecker structure to accelerate computations and reduce memory requirements for data arranged on a partial grid while allowing for missing values.
In contrast to existing Gaussian process models with Kronecker structure, our approach deals with missing values by combining projections and iterative linear algebra methods.
Empirically, we demonstrated that our method has superior computational scalability compared to standard iterative methods, and substantially outperforms sparse and variational Gaussian processes in terms of prediction quality.
Future work could investigate specialised kernels, multi-product generalisations, and heteroscedastic noise models.

6.4.1
Limitations

The primary limitation of LKGP is that it employs a product kernel, which assumes that points are only highly correlated if they are close in both
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{T}
.
While this is a reasonable assumption in many real-world settings, such as correlated time series data, it will not always be appropriate.
The other requirement for LKGP to be applicable is that the data lives on at least a partial Cartesian product grid.
This requirement is less restrictive since our model is highly competitive even if there are a lot of missing values, as our experiments and theory show.
Furthermore, if the data does not even live on a partial grid, it would be possible to generate an artificial grid, for example via local interpolation.

Chapter 7
Conclusion

In this dissertation, we approached the topic of scalable Gaussian processes from a modern perspective, involving large datasets and hardware for massively-parallel computation.
We developed methods which are able to scale to such modern circumstances by focusing on the effective combination of iterative methods and pathwise conditioning.
In particular, all of our contributions are generally about reducing asymptotic time and memory requirements, leveraging fast matrix multiplication, improving the speed of convergence, and analysing the behaviour under non-convergence.
I hope that our contributions facilitate the use of Gaussian processes in modern large-scale settings.

In the following, I summarise the original contributions and insights of this dissertation:

•

Chapter

3
introduced stochastic gradient descent as an iterative linear system solver to approximately compute samples from the Gaussian process posterior via pathwise conditioning.
To this end, we derived custom low-variance optimisation objectives, which we further extended to inducing points for even greater scalability.
A key insight from this chapter is the observation that
stochastic gradient descent can produce accurate predictions even if it did not converge to the optimum
.
To explain this phenomenon, we developed a spectral characterisation of the effects of non-convergence, showing that the error under non-convergence mainly manifests at the boundaries of the observed data.
Empirically, we evaluated our proposed algorithm on various regression tasks, achieving state-of-the-art performance for sufficiently large or ill-conditioned settings.
Furthermore, we demonstrated that, at a fraction of the computational cost, stochastic gradient descent matches or exceeds the performance of other more computationally expensive baseline methods on a large-scale Bayesian optimisation task.

•

Chapter

4
improved the proposed stochastic gradient descent algorithm from the previous chapter by combining various ideas from the optimisation and kernel communities.
In particular, we introduced a dual optimisation objective, which facilitates larger step sizes due to more favourable curvature properties, leading to significantly faster convergence and reduced computational costs.
Additionally, we analysed two different ways of performing stochastic gradient estimation, resulting in the important insight that
the estimator with multiplicative noise, which decreases as the optimisation approaches the optimum, provides substantially improved convergence properties compared to the estimator with constant additive noise
.
Furthermore, we investigated momentum and iterate averaging techniques, leading to recommendations with lower variance and superior convergence properties.
Empirically, we improved upon the performance from the previous chapter, and further demonstrated that our method achieves state-of-the-art results on a molecular binding affinity prediction task, matching graph neural networks.

•

Chapter

5
proposed generic contributions towards iterative linear system solvers for marginal likelihood optimisation.
We introduced a pathwise gradient estimator, which reduces the required number of solver iterations until convergence, and leverages a connection to pathwise conditioning to amortise the computational costs of making predictions.
Additionally, we used intermediate solutions to warm start iterative linear system solvers, leading to faster convergence at the cost of introducing negligible bias.
Furthermore, we investigated the behaviour of iterative linear system solvers under a limited computational budget, terminating optimisation before reaching convergence.
Notably, we observed that,
in certain cases, a small compute budget was sufficient to achieve good predictive performance, despite not reaching convergence
.
Empirically, we showed that our techniques provide speed-ups of up to
72\times
when solving until convergence, and decrease the average residual norm by up to
7\times
when stopping early.

•

Chapter

6
developed latent Kronecker structure for Gaussian process regression with Kronecker product-structured kernel matrices.
The latter enables scalable inference via factorised matrix decompositions, but typically requires fully gridded data from a Cartesian product space to be applicable.
To support incomplete grids, we expressed the kernel matrix as the projection of a latent Kronecker product, and leveraged fast matrix multiplication in combination with iterative methods and pathwise conditioning to perform scalable inference.
In particular,
the latent factorisation drastically reduces the memory requirements, allowing kernel values to be precomputed, which significantly reduces the overall computational costs
.
On real-world datasets with up to five million examples, our method outperformed state-of-the-art sparse and variational approaches.

7.1
Future Directions

Finally, I will discuss potential directions for future research, based on the current limitations of our contributions.
Personally, I prefer to be optimistic about limitations and think of them more as opportunities for improvement rather than definitive weaknesses.
In this spirit, I will discuss some limitations of the methodological contributions of this dissertation, along with potential avenues for future work.

Adaptive Learning Rate Selection for Stochastic Gradient Descent

Stochastic gradient and dual descent (
Chapters

3
and
4
) in their current form have several free hyperparameters, such as the learning rate, batch size, or momentum and iterate averaging parameters, which is inconvenient for any practitioner who simply wants to use them as a scalable black-box linear system solver.
In particular, the learning rate, also referred to as the step size, has a strong influence on the rate of convergence.
In combination with noise and kernel hyperparameter optimisation, the problem is further exacerbated, because the optimal learning rate depends on their values.
Therefore, future research could consider methods to select the learning rate in an automatic way
(
Defazio & Mishchenko 2023
)
.
If successful, this would greatly improve the convenience and practicality of these algorithms.

Understanding and Leveraging Non-Convergence Properties

Throughout this dissertation, we have repeatedly observed that terminating iterative linear system solvers before reaching convergence does not necessarily lead to worse performance.
While
Chapter

3
provided some theoretical arguments in the context of drawing posterior samples with stochastic gradient descent, the relationship between relative residual norm convergence and kernel hyperparameter optimisation in
Chapter

5
is unclear.
Future work could further investigate how the residual norm relates to the objective of marginal likelihood optimisation.
My impression is that the standard Euclidean norm of the residual, which is commonly used in combination with iterative linear system solvers, is suboptimal, because it does not depend on the spectrum of the kernel matrix.
Since the solutions of the linear systems are used to calculate inner products with respect to the derivative of the kernel matrix, I would expect the latter to play an important role in determining convergence.
The question then becomes how to formulate a convergence criterion which adequately reflects this while also being amenable to tractable, and ideally amortised, computation.
An improved convergence criterion could inform the design of new solvers which directly optimise for it.

Latent Kronecker Structure of Higher Order and General Purpose

Chapter

6
proposed the idea of expressing a covariance matrix as the projection of a latent Kronecker product to leverage fast matrix multiplication in the latent space while reducing memory requirements.
Although we only considered latent Kronecker products with two factors, it would be straightforward to generalise this idea to an arbitrary number of factors, as long as the data continues to be a subset of a corresponding Cartesian product space.
For example, in our large-scale experiment on climate data, we factorised the data into space and time.
With a multi-product generalisation, the space coordinates could be further factorised into latitudes and longitudes.
Furthermore, the concept of latent Kronecker structure could be applied to other applications.
Future work could, for example, investigate latent Kronecker structure for second-order optimisation methods.
The latter typically need to represent the Hessian or inverse Hessian matrix of the objective function, which can quickly become intractable due to quadratically increasing memory requirements.
Modern approaches for deep learning already leverage Kronecker factorisation
(
Eschenhagen et al. 2023
)
, such that latent Kronecker structure could be a natural extension.

Gaussian Processes in the Era of Deep Learning

Last but not least, I want to share my thoughts about Gaussian processes in the era of deep learning.
At machine learning conferences, I have occasionally been confronted by fellow researchers with the question:

‘If you could scale Gaussian processes to arbitrary amounts of data, would you use them instead of neural networks?’

Typically, this question was not asked in a derogatory way, but rather out of curiosity, and my answer would be:
‘It depends…’

Aside from uncertainty quantification capabilities, which I mentioned in
Chapter

1
, Gaussian processes and neural networks follow a fundamentally different approach when specifying a hypothesis space of latent functions.
The former is fully specified by explicit mean and covariance functions, whereas the latter explores a potentially much larger space which is implicitly defined by its architecture.
Some may argue that the latter is more powerful since it can adapt to the data, which allows neural networks to learn remarkably complex functions.
However, if the class of latent functions is known, one can explicitly choose, or even design
(
Swersky et al. 2014
)
, a covariance function which incorporates this information.
I believe that this approach capitalises on the advantages of Gaussian processes and should thus be pursued instead of competing with neural networks to learn complex implicit functions.
Will Gaussian processes ever be able to generate photorealistic images of cute cats or have a fluent conversation in a natural language?
Most likely not.
Should we still cherish them? Yes.

Appendix A
Mathematical Derivations

This appendix contains mathematical derivations which are too verbose to be included in the main body of this dissertation.
The following derivations are based on and adapted from:

•

J. A. Lin, J. Antorán, S. Padhy, D. Janz, J. M. Hernández-Lobato, and A. Terenin. Sampling from Gaussian Process Posteriors using Stochastic Gradient Descent. In
Advances in Neural Information Processing Systems
, 2023.

A.1
The Implicit Bias of Stochastic Gradient Descent

This section provides the main theoretical results of
Chapter

3
, including
Proposition

3.1
.

A.1.1
Convergence of Stochastic Gradient Descent

Before studying what happens in Gaussian processes, we first prove a fairly standard result on the convergence of SGD for a quadratic objective appearing in kernel ridge regression, which represents the posterior mean.
For simplicity, we do not analyse Nesterov momentum nor aspects such as gradient clipping.
Recall that a random variable
z
is called
G
-sub-Gaussian
if
\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}(\exp(\lambda(z-\operatorname{\mathbb{E}}(z))))\leq\exp(\smash{\frac{1}{2}}G\lambda^{2})
holds for all
\lambda
.
Furthermore, a random vector is called
G
-sub-Gaussian if its dot product with any unit vector is
G
-sub-Gaussian.
One can show this condition is equivalent to having tails that are no heavier than those of a Gaussian random vector, formulated appropriately.
Let
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}
be the eigenvalue decomposition of the kernel matrix with eigenvectors
\bm{u}_{i}
and eigenvalues
\lambda_{1}\geq...\geq\lambda_{n}>0
.

Lemma A.1
.

Let
\delta>0
and noise variance
\sigma^{2}>0
.
Let
\eta
be a sufficiently small learning rate of
0<\eta<\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1}+\sigma^{2})}
.
Let
\bm{v}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be the solution of the respective linear system, and let
\overline{\bm{v}}_{t}
be the Polyak-averaged SGD iterate after
t
steps, starting from an initial condition of
\bm{v}_{0}=\bm{0}
.
Assume that the stochastic estimate of the gradient is unbiased and
G
-sub-Gaussian. Then, with probability
1-\delta
, we have for any
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,...,n\}
and all
i\in\mathcal{I}
that

|\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\overline{\bm{v}}_{t})|\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{2}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\sigma^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}}\right),

(A.1)

Proof.

Consider the objective

L(\bm{v})=\frac{1}{2}\left\lVert\bm{y}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{v}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}+\frac{\sigma^{2}}{2}\left\lVert\bm{v}\right\rVert_{\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}}^{2},

(A.2)

and respective gradient

\frac{\partial L}{\partial\bm{v}}(\bm{v})=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}^{2}\bm{v}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{v}=\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})\bm{v}-\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}.

(A.3)

Let us first look at non-stochastic gradient optimisation of
L
, without Polyak averaging.
The iterate
\bm{v}_{t}
is given by

\bm{v}_{t}=\bm{v}_{t-1}-\eta\frac{\partial L}{\partial\bm{v}}(\bm{v}_{t-1})=\left(\mathbf{I}-\eta\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})\right)\bm{v}_{t-1}+\eta\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}.

(A.4)

Writing
\mathbf{M}=\left(\mathbf{I}-\eta\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})\right)
, and recalling that
\bm{v}_{0}=\bm{0}
, we thus have that

\bm{v}_{t}=\eta\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\mathbf{M}^{j}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}=\eta(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{M})^{-1}(\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{M}^{t})\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{y}=\bm{v}^{*}-(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{M}^{t}\bm{y},

(A.5)

where, for our choice of
\eta
, by direct calculation using simultaneous diagonalisability of
\mathbf{M}
and
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}
, the eigenvalues of
\mathbf{M}
are strictly between
0
and
1
.
Therefore, the geometric series converges and
\mathbf{I}-\mathbf{M}
is positive definite.
Examining the error in direction
\bm{u}_{i}
, we see that

|\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\bm{v}_{t})|=|\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}+\sigma^{2}\mathbf{I})^{-1}\mathbf{M}^{t}\bm{y}|\leq\frac{\left(1-\eta\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\right)^{t}}{\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2}}\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2},

(A.6)

which applies to ordinary gradient descent without stochastic gradients or Polyak averaging.
Next, we consider stochastic gradient optimisation.
We will first consider the case where the gradient is independently perturbed by
\bm{\zeta}_{t}\sim\mathcal{N}(\bm{0},\mathbf{I})
for each step
t>0
, and then relax this to sub-Gaussian noise in the sequel.

Specifically, we consider

\bm{v}^{\prime}_{t}=\bm{v}^{\prime}_{t-1}-\eta\left(\frac{\partial L}{\partial\bm{v}}(\bm{v}^{\prime}_{t-1})+\bm{\zeta}_{t}\right)=\bm{v}_{t}-\eta\sum_{j=0}^{t-1}\mathbf{M}^{j}\bm{\zeta}_{t-j},

(A.7)

with
\bm{v}^{\prime}_{0}=\bm{v}_{0}=\bm{0}
.
For these iterates, we consider the respective Polyak-averaged iterates denoted by
\overline{\bm{v}}_{t}=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\bm{v}^{\prime}_{j}
.
We have

|\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\overline{\bm{v}}_{t})|=\Bigg|\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\bm{v}^{\prime}_{j})\Bigg|\leq\Bigg|\underbracket{\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\bm{v}_{j})}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{A_{i,t}}\vphantom{|}}\Bigg|+\Bigg|\underbracket{\frac{1}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{(}\bm{v}_{j}-\bm{v}^{\prime}_{j})}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{B_{i,t}}\vphantom{|}}\Bigg|,

(A.8)

by expanding the Polyak averages and applying the triangle inequality.
Using
Equation

A.6
, the triangle inequality and another geometric series, we can bound the first sum as

|A_{i,t}|=\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\left(1-\eta\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\right)^{j}\leq\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})^{2}t}.

(A.9)

For the second sum, we observe that we can change the order of summation to count each
\bm{\zeta}_{j}
only once, rather than once for each Polyak average.
This gives

\displaystyle B_{i,t}

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\sum_{q=0}^{j-1}\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{M}^{q}\bm{\zeta}_{j-q}=\frac{\eta}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\left(\sum_{q=0}^{t-j}\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{M}^{q}\right)\bm{\zeta}_{j},

(A.10)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\sum_{q=0}^{t-j}\left(1-\eta\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\right)^{q}\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\zeta}_{j}.

(A.11)

where
\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\zeta}_{j}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)
are independent and identically distributed.

The variance of
B_{i,t}
can be bounded by using another geometric series

\displaystyle\Var(B_{i,t})

\displaystyle=\Var\left(\frac{\eta}{t}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\sum_{q=0}^{t-j}\left(1-\eta\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\right)^{q}\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\zeta}_{j}\right),

(A.12)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{2}}{t^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\Var\left(\sum_{q=0}^{t-j}\left(1-\eta\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\right)^{q}\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\zeta}_{j}\right),

(A.13)

\displaystyle=\frac{\eta^{2}}{t^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\left[\left(\sum_{q=0}^{t-j}\left(1-\eta\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})\right)^{q}\right)^{2}\underbracket{\Var\left(\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\zeta}_{j}\right)}_{\vphantom{|}\mathclap{=1}\vphantom{|}}\right],

(A.14)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\eta^{2}}{t^{2}}\sum_{j=1}^{t}\left(\frac{1}{\eta\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})}\right)^{2}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{2}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})^{2}t}.

(A.15)

Thus, from standard tail inequalities for Gaussian random variables, for any
\delta^{\prime}>0
, we have

\operatorname{\mathbb{P}}\left(|B_{i,t}|\leq\sqrt{\frac{2}{\lambda_{i}^{2}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})^{2}t}\log\frac{1}{\delta^{\prime}}}\right)\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}.

(A.16)

We then take
\delta^{\prime}=\delta/|\mathcal{I}|
and apply the union bound for all indices in
\mathcal{I}
.
Finally, by comparing moment generating functions, we can relax the Gaussian assumption to
G
-sub-Gaussian random variables.
To complete the claim, we combine the bounds for the two sums as

\displaystyle|\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\overline{\bm{v}}_{t})|

\displaystyle\leq|A_{i,t}|+|B_{i,t}|,

(A.17)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{\lambda_{i}^{2}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})^{2}t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}},

(A.18)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta(\lambda_{i}+\sigma^{2})t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}}\right),

(A.19)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{2}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\sigma^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}}\right),

(A.20)

which gives the claim.
∎

A.1.2
Convergence in Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space

Using the preceding result, we will show that SGD converges quickly with respect to a certain seminorm.
Let
k
be the kernel and let
\mathcal{H}_{k}
be its associated reproducing kernel Hilbert space.

Definition A.1
.

Let
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Lambda}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}
be the eigendecomposition of the kernel matrix.
Define the spectral basis functions as

u^{(i)}(\cdot)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\frac{U_{ji}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}}k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{j}).

(A.21)

These are precisely the basis functions which arise in kernel principal component analysis.
We also introduce two subspaces of the RKHS: the span of the representer weights, and the span of a subset of spectral basis functions.
These are defined as follows.

Definition A.2
.

Define the representer weight space

R_{k,\mathbf{X}}=\Span\big\{k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i}):i=1,...,n\big\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}_{k},

(A.22)

equipped with the subspace inner product.

Definition A.3
.

Let
\mathcal{I}\subseteq\{1,...,n\}
be an arbitrary set of indices.
Define the interpolation subspace

R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}=\Span\big\{u^{(i)}:i\in\mathcal{I}\big\}\subseteq R_{k,\mathbf{X}}.

(A.23)

We can use these subspaces to define a seminorm on the RKHS, which we will use to measure convergence rates of SGD.

Definition A.4
.

Define the interpolation seminorm

\left|f\right|_{R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}}=\lVert\proj_{R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}}f\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}.

(A.24)

Here,
\proj_{\mathcal{S}}f
denotes orthogonal projection of a function
f
onto the subspace
\mathcal{S}
of a Hilbert space.
To ease notation, in cases where we project onto the span of a single vector, we write the vector in place of
\mathcal{S}
.
The first order of business is to understand the relationship between the spectral basis functions and representer weight space.

Lemma A.2
.

The functions
u^{(i)}
with
i=1,...,n
form an orthonormal basis of
R_{k,\mathbf{X}}
.

Proof.

Let
i\neq j
.
Using the reproducing property, the definition of an eigenvector, the fact that
\bm{u}_{i}
and
\bm{u}_{j}
are eigenvectors of
\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}
, and orthonormality of eigenvectors, we have

\langle u^{(i)},u^{(j)}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\frac{\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{u}_{j}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}}=\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}\lambda_{j}}}\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{u}_{j}=0.

(A.25)

The fact that
u^{(i)}
form a basis follows from the fact that they are linearly independent, that there are
n
of them in total, and that this equals the dimensionality of
R_{k,\mathbf{X}}
by definition.
Finally, we calculate the norm

\lVert u^{(i)}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\frac{\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{u}_{i}}{\lambda_{i}}=1,

(A.26)

which gives the claim.
∎

Next, we derive a change-of-basis formula between the canonical and spectral basis functions.

Lemma A.3
.

For some
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, let
\theta(\cdot)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\theta_{i}k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}u^{(j)}(\cdot)
.
Then we have

\bm{w}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\theta}\quad\text{ and }\quad\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{w}.

(A.27)

Proof.

By expanding
u^{(j)}
, we have

\theta(\cdot)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}u^{(j)}(\cdot)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}\frac{U_{ij}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i}).

(A.28)

Since
k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})
form a basis, this implies
\theta_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}\frac{U_{ij}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}
, or equivalently
\bm{\theta}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{w}
and
\bm{w}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\theta}
.
From orthonormality of
u^{(j)}
, we conclude that

\left\lVert\theta\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\left\langle\theta,\theta\right\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{i}w_{j}\langle u^{(i)},u^{(j)}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}^{2}=\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{w},

(A.29)

which gives the second claim.
∎

This allows us to get an explicit expression for the previously introduced seminorm.

Lemma A.4
.

For
\theta\in R_{k,\mathbf{X}}
, letting
\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{I}}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1}\mathbbold{1}_{1\in\mathcal{I}},...,\lambda_{i}\mathbbold{1}_{i\in\mathcal{I}},...,\lambda_{n}\mathbbold{1}_{n\in\mathcal{I}})
, we have

\left|\theta\right|_{R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}}^{2}=\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{I}}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\theta}.

(A.30)

Proof.

First, we decompose
\theta
in the orthonormal basis above, giving
\theta(\cdot)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}w_{j}u^{(j)}(\cdot)
.
By orthonormality, the definition of
R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}
, and properties of projections, we have

(\proj_{R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}}\theta)(\cdot)=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}w_{j}u^{(j)}(\cdot).

(A.31)

Therefore, again using orthonormality, we obtain

\left|\theta\right|_{R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}}^{2}=\sum_{j\in\mathcal{I}}w_{j}^{2}=\bm{\theta}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{I}}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{\theta},

(A.32)

which gives the claim.
∎

We claim that our variant of SGD converges quickly with respect to this seminorm.

Proposition A.1
.

Let
\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}
be the exact posterior predictive mean.
Let
\hat{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}
be the posterior predictive mean obtained by SGD under the assumptions of
Lemma

A.1
.
For any
\mathcal{I}
, we have

\left|\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}-\hat{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}\right|_{R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}}\leq\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\sigma^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}}\right)\sqrt{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{3}}}.

(A.33)

Proof.

Using
Lemma

A.4
and linearity, we have

\displaystyle\left|\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}-\hat{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}\right|_{R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}}

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{i=1}^{n}(v^{*}_{i}-\hat{v}_{i})k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})\right|_{R_{k,\mathbf{X}}^{\mathcal{I}}},

(A.34)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{(\bm{v}^{*}-\hat{\bm{v}})^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{I}}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\hat{\bm{v}})},

(A.35)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\left|\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\hat{\bm{v}})\right|^{2}\lambda_{i}},

(A.36)

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\left|\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{2}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\sigma^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}}\right)\right|^{2}\lambda_{i}},

(A.37)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\sigma^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}}\right)\sqrt{\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{3}}},

(A.38)

where the inequality is obtained using the result of
Lemma

A.1
, yielding the claim.
∎

Our main claim follows directly from the established framework.
See
3.1

Proof.

Choose
\mathcal{I}
to be a singleton and apply
Proposition

A.1
.
Replacing
|\mathcal{I}|
with
n
in the final bound makes the claim hold with probability
1-\delta
for all
i=1,...,n
simultaneously.
∎

Therefore, SGD converges fast with respect to this seminorm.
In particular, taking
\mathcal{I}
to be the full index set, a position-dependent pointwise convergence bound follows.

Corollary A.1
.

Under the assumptions of
Lemma

A.1
, we have

|\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}-\hat{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}|\leq\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\sigma^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}^{3}}}|u^{(i)}(\cdot)|.

(A.39)

Proof.

Using
Lemma

A.3
, we write

\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}-\hat{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(v^{*}_{j}-\hat{v}_{j})k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(w^{*}_{i}-\hat{w}_{i})u^{(i)}(\cdot),

(A.40)

where
\bm{w}^{*}-\hat{\bm{w}}=\mathbf{\Lambda}^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbf{U}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\hat{\bm{v}})
.
Applying
Lemma

A.1
with
\mathcal{I}=\{1,...,n\}
, we conclude

\displaystyle|\mu_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}-\hat{\mu}_{(\cdot)\mid\bm{y}}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}|w^{*}_{i}-\hat{w}_{i}||u^{(i)}(\cdot)|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}|\bm{u}_{i}^{\mathsf{T}}(\bm{v}^{*}-\hat{\bm{v}})||u^{(i)}(\cdot)|,

(A.41)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}\left|\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}^{2}}\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\sigma^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}}\right)\right||u^{(i)}(\cdot)|,

(A.42)

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{\left\lVert\bm{y}\right\rVert_{2}}{\eta\sigma^{2}t}+G\sqrt{\frac{2}{t}\log\frac{|\mathcal{I}|}{\delta}}\right)\sum_{i=1}^{n}\frac{1}{\sqrt{\lambda_{i}^{3}}}|u^{(i)}(\cdot)|,

(A.43)

which gives the claim.
∎

Examining the expression, the approximation error will be high at locations
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{X}
where
u^{(i)}(\bm{x})
corresponding to tail eigenfunctions is large.
We proceed to analyse when this occurs.

A.1.3
Courant-Fischer in the Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space

Since the preceding seminorm is induced by a projection within the RKHS, we want to understand what kind of functions the corresponding subspace contains.
To get a qualitative view, the basic idea is to lift the Courant-Fischer characterisation of eigenvalues and eigenvectors to the RKHS.
To this end, we will need the following variant of the min-max theorem, which is useful for simultaneously describing multiple eigenvectors as solutions to a sequence of Rayleigh quotient optimisation problems, where the next optimisation problem is performed on a subspace which ensures its solution is orthogonal to all previous solutions.
As before, we consider eigenvalues in decreasing order, namely
\lambda_{1}\geq..\geq\lambda_{n}\geq 0
.

Corollary A.2
.

Let
\mathbf{A}
be a positive semi-definite matrix.
Then

\lambda_{i}=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\\
\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{u}_{j}=0,\forall j<i\end{subarray}}\frac{\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{A}\bm{u}}{\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{u}},

(A.44)

where the eigenvectors
\bm{u}_{i}
are the respective maximisers.

Proof.

Horn & Johnson 2012
, modified appropriately to handle eigenvalues in decreasing order, and where we choose
i_{1},...,i_{k}=1,...,n-i+1
.
∎

To prove the main claim, we first consider the representer weight space
R_{k,\mathbf{X}}
and then
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.

Lemma A.5
.

We have

u^{(i)}(\cdot)=\argmax_{u\in R_{k,\mathbf{X}}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}u(\bm{x}_{i})^{2}:\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=1,\langle u,u^{(j)}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=0,\forall j<i\right\}.

(A.45)

Proof.

Define
\bm{w}_{j}=\mathbf{U}\mathbf{\Lambda}^{-\frac{1}{2}}\bm{u}_{j}
, where we recall that
\bm{u}_{j}
are the eigenvectors of the kernel matrix.
From
Corollary

A.2
, the reproducing property, and the explicit form of the RKHS inner product on
R_{k,\mathbf{X}}
in the basis defined by the canonical basis functions, we conclude that

\displaystyle\lambda_{i}

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\\
\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{u}_{j}=0,\forall j<i\end{subarray}}\frac{\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{u}}{\bm{u}^{\mathsf{T}}\bm{u}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\\
\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{w}_{j}=0,\forall j<i\end{subarray}}\frac{\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}^{2}\bm{w}}{\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{w}},

(A.46)

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\\
\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{w}_{j}=0,\forall j<i\end{subarray}}\frac{\left\lVert\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{w}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{w}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}\bm{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\{\bm{0}\}\\
\bm{w}^{\mathsf{T}}\mathbf{K}_{\mathbf{X}\mathbf{X}}\bm{w}_{j}=0,\forall j<i\end{subarray}}\frac{\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}k(\mathbf{X},\bm{x}_{i})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\left\lVert\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}},

(A.47)

\displaystyle=\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in R_{k,\mathbf{X}}\setminus\{0\}\\
\langle u,u^{(j)}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=0,\forall j<i\end{subarray}}\frac{\left\lVert u(\mathbf{X})\right\rVert_{2}^{2}}{\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\max_{\begin{subarray}{c}u\in R_{k,\mathbf{X}}\\
\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=1\\
\langle u,u^{(j)}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=0,\forall j<i\end{subarray}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}u(\bm{x}_{i})^{2},

(A.48)

which gives the claim.
∎

Next, we use an orthogonality argument, which relies on the projection theorem for Hilbert spaces, to extend the optimisation problem to
\mathcal{H}_{k}
.

Proposition A.2
.

We have

u^{(i)}(\cdot)=\argmax_{u\in\mathcal{H}_{k}}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}u(\bm{x}_{i})^{2}:\left\lVert u\right\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=1,\langle u,u^{(j)}\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=0,\forall j<i\right\}.

(A.49)

Proof.

Let
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}
be the orthogonal complement of the subspace
\Span\{u^{(j)},j=1,...,i-1\}
in
\mathcal{H}_{k}
and note by feasibility that
u^{(i)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}
.
Consider the decomposition of
u^{(i)}
into its projection onto an orthogonal complement with respect to the finite-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}\cap R_{k,\mathbf{X}}
, namely

u^{(i)}(\cdot)=u^{\parallel}(\cdot)+u^{\perp}(\cdot),

(A.50)

where this notation is defined as
u^{\parallel}=\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}\cap R_{k,\mathbf{X}}}u^{(i)}
and
u^{\perp}=u^{(i)}-u^{\parallel}
.
Observe that

u^{\perp}(\bm{x}_{i})=\langle u^{\perp},k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\langle\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}}u^{\perp},k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=\langle u^{\perp},\proj_{\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}}k(\cdot,\bm{x}_{i})\rangle_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}=0,

(A.51)

where the first equality follows from the reproducing property, the second equality follows because
u^{\perp}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}
, the third equality follows since the projection is orthogonal and thus self-adjoint, and the final equality follows because
u^{\perp}
by definition lives in the orthogonal complement of
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}\cap R_{k,\mathbf{X}}
within
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}
, with the subspace inner product.
Moreover, by the projection theorem and the fact that
\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}
inherits its norm from
\mathcal{H}_{k}
, note that

\lVert u^{(i)}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}=\lVert u^{\parallel}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}+\lVert u^{\perp}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}.

(A.52)

Suppose that
u^{\perp}\neq 0
.
This implies that
\lVert u^{\perp}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}>0
.
From the above, we have

\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{i})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{\parallel}(\bm{x}_{i})^{2}\quad\text{ and }\quad\lVert u^{\parallel}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}<\lVert u^{(i)}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}.

(A.53)

Define the function

u^{\prime}(\cdot)=\frac{\lVert u^{(i)}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}}{\lVert u^{\parallel}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}}u^{\parallel}(\cdot),

(A.54)

and note that
\left\lVert u^{\prime}\right\rVert=1
, and
\langle u^{\prime},u^{(j)}\rangle=0
for all
j<i
since
u^{\parallel}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}
, which makes
u^{\prime}
a feasible solution to the optimisation problem considered.
At the same time, it satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{\prime}(\bm{x}_{i})^{2}=\frac{\lVert u^{(i)}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}}{\lVert u^{\parallel}\rVert_{\mathcal{H}_{k}}^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{\parallel}(\bm{x}_{i})^{2}>\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{\parallel}(\bm{x}_{i})^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}u^{(i)}(\bm{x}_{i})^{2},

(A.55)

which shows that
u^{(i)}
, contrary to its definition, is not actually optimal.
We conclude that
u^{\perp}=0
, which means that
u^{(i)}\in\mathcal{H}_{k}^{(i)}\cap R_{k,\mathbf{X}}
.
The claim follows from
Lemma

A.5
.
∎

This means that the top eigenvalues can be understood as the largest possible squared sums of canonical basis functions evaluated at the data, subject to RKHS-norm constraints.
In situations where
k(\cdot,\bm{x})
is continuous, non-negative, and decays as one moves away from the data, it is intuitively clear that such sums will be maximised by placing weight on canonical basis functions which cover as much of the data as possible.
This mirrors the RKHS view of kernel principal component analysis.
